| BUG-242 | **High** / spec P3, AC_SECURITY (an abandoned tab could take the account over) | closed | round-4 hunt (`wf_8f2c40ab-4dd`), fixed in `wf_b0dabd41-8b0` (WEB-R4-AUTH-1) — a defect in the lead's own round-3 fix | Expected: only a session that came from the emailed link may set a password with nothing else, and that grant lasts one change and fifteen minutes (BUG-175). Actual: on the mobile-link path the page ORed its own local `recovery.status === 'ready'` in front of `recoveryActive()`, and that flag is written once by the adopt effect and never reset — so an `/update-password` tab left open after a phone-requested reset still changed the account password with no current password, hours later. The bound the lead added in round 3 was unreachable on exactly the path round 2c had built | "One bounded grant in the adapter is enough" — rejected: a second, unbounded grant in the page's own state sat in front of it, and the round-3 tests only exercised the adapter | `fromRecoveryLink` now reads `recoverySession?.recoveryActive() === true` alone; `acceptRecoveryLink` already opens the bounded grant, so both paths share one source of truth, one use and one deadline | `apps/web/src/pages/auth/UpdatePasswordPage.reauth.test.tsx` 'an abandoned mobile-link tab is not a standing grant' → 'asks for the current password once the fifteen minutes have passed' (plus the inside-the-window case); failed before the change | — |
| BUG-243 | **High** / AC_SECURITY_02 (a newer safety flag could be hidden for good) | closed | round-4 hunt (`wf_8f2c40ab-4dd`), fixed in `wf_b0dabd41-8b0` (CS-R4-01) — a defect in the lead's own round-3 fix | Expected: a flag waiting on a grown-up is always in the family's list (BUG-188). Actual: the oldest-first bound only drains for rows somebody can resolve, and a parent cannot resolve a report they filed — `PATCH /v1/safety-reports/:id` refuses it, only staff can. 200 open parent reports therefore pinned the page, and a NEW system flag never reached the family at all: worse, for that case, than the newest-first cap it replaced | "An oldest-first bound always drains" — rejected by the round-4 finder: it drains only through an action path, and the lead's own regression test had hidden that by resolving rows with raw SQL while stating that the action path was not under test | The unresolved rows are bounded PER REPORTER KIND: system flags oldest-first (a guardian can address or clear those, so that bound really drains), child-filed and parent-filed reports newest-first in their own branches, plus the newest resolved as before. A reporter kind added later falls into the catch-all branch and is listed rather than silently dropped | `apps/api/tests/privacy-r2.review.test.ts` 'a new flag while the family already filed 200+ reports of its own' → 'lists a newer flag and a newer child report behind 200+ older open parent reports'; the lead's round-3 case was rewritten as 'bounds the unresolved rows per reporter kind, and the flag still drains' and no longer resolves anything by raw SQL; failed before the change | Lesson L-046: a bound drains only through an action path, and a fixture shortcut that bypasses that path proves the property you designed for instead of the one the family needs |
| BUG-244 | **High** / AC_ACCESS_04 (one lost response unpairs a child’s tablet) | **REOPENED — accepted, documented open defect** | round-4 hunt (`wf_8f2c40ab-4dd`) (HUNT4-MOB-1); held open through round 4 by the lead’s decision, then designed and fixed by the lead in the round after it (migration 0880) | Expected: a child keeps working while the pairing is valid. Actual: `/v1/child/refresh` stamps the rotation in the same transaction that produces the response, so a response lost on the way back leaves the tablet holding a token the server has rotated. Its next refresh is read as reuse — the theft signal — and the session is revoked, so one dropped HTTP response unpairs the device and a parent must mint a new pairing code | (a) A 60-second “the response never arrived” grace window, written in the fix round and withdrawn by its own author — REFUSED by the lead: a time window cannot tell the rightful holder’s retry from a blind replay of a stolen token, so it would hand a replayer a live child session in exactly the case it exists to help (lesson L-047). (b) Keying the recovery on the token itself — rejected: a replayer presents exactly that. (c) Re-serving the replacement token that the lost response carried — rejected: only its hash is stored, and storing it in the clear to make that possible would be worse than the bug | The request names itself. `refreshRequestId` (optional, uuid) on `POST /v1/child/refresh`; migration 0880 adds a nullable `private.child_refresh_tokens.used_request_id` with a partial index and no client-role grant; rotation records the id on the row it retires. A presentation of an already-rotated token is recovery rather than theft only when all of: it carries an id AND that id is the one which consumed this token; the replacement that rotation produced is still unused and unexpired (`for update`); the session is live and consent still allows access. Recovery then retires that replacement at the ORIGINAL rotation instant (so it gets no window of its own), issues a fresh token, re-points the chain and logs `child_refresh_recovered` / `LOST_RESPONSE`. Every other presentation still revokes the session, including one with no id, one with a different id, and a replay after the replacement has been used. On the device the id is minted once per refresh, written to secure storage BEFORE the request goes out and cleared only after the rotated token is stored — so an app the OS kills mid-refresh finishes that same refresh on its next cold start; `unpairChildDevice` drops it with the session, and a stored value that is not a uuid is replaced rather than presented forever | `apps/api/tests/mobile-r2.review.test.ts` ‘a refresh whose response was lost is recoverable by its own id (BUG-244)’ — 5 cases: the repro (same token, same id → tokens, not a revocation), a second lost response in a row, a replay with a different id and one with no id at all (both theft), a replay after the replacement was used (theft), and a client that sends no id keeping today’s behaviour exactly. `apps/mobile/src/family/child-session-r2.review.test.ts` ‘a refresh keeps its request id across this device’s own retries (BUG-244)’ — 5 cases incl. the cold start after a process kill and the non-uuid stored value. `supabase/tests/hardening_r1_db2.test.ts` pins the column, the partial index and the absent client grant. Mutation checks: dropping `row.used_request_id === refreshRequestId` from the server guard fails ‘a replay with a different id is theft, and so is one with no id at all’ (200 where 401 is required); dropping the `setItem` of the id fails ‘a process killed mid-refresh finishes that same refresh after a cold start’. Both files restored byte-for-byte (`sha256sum -c`) | Residual, deliberate: a full man-in-the-middle who captures the lost RESPONSE already holds the replacement token, so the id gives such an attacker nothing new; and a device that never retries (uninstall, storage wipe) still needs a new pairing code, which is correct. The parent-side story is unchanged: a genuinely stolen token still ends the session Also deliberate: two presentations of the same token with the same id arriving CONCURRENTLY still end the session — the first retires the replacement under `for update`, so the second finds it used and cannot tell itself from a replay racing the holder. The device single-flights its refresh (`child-session.ts`, L-007), so this needs a client that sends the same attempt twice at once; failing closed there is the right default, and chasing the recovery chain further would trade clarity for a case the client does not produce. SUPERSEDED IN ROUND 5, by its own defects: the hunt over this change found that "the replacement is unclaimed" is NOT "the response was lost" (BUG-248, High), that the retired replacement inherited the request id and was a second recovery key (BUG-249), and that the device kept the id across a session’s end (BUG-286). As shipped at `b802686` the recovery is additionally bounded to two minutes from the rotation — ANDed with the id, never ORed — and writes an audit event, the retired replacement carries no id, and the device drops a stored id older than that window. Read those four rows together: this one records the design, they record what was wrong with it. REOPENED at `5ba16c3`: the recovery that closed this row was REMOVED in round 6 (BUG-294 / HUNT6-A-1). It produced four findings in two days and its recorded residual understated the exposure — a recovery never marked the row it recovered, so one captured request body was served repeatedly for the whole window and each serving returned a full-lifetime rotating refresh token that then rotated on with no id, no window and no audit row, which is a self-renewing child session until the tablet's next refresh, not 'served once'. Migration 0880 was deleted rather than dropped (no deployed database ever had the column). So the ORIGINAL defect stands and is accepted: a lost /v1/child/refresh response unpairs a child's tablet and a parent mints a new pairing code. Owner action #45 is restored. What is NOT accepted, and is why this is the right end state, is the trade the recovery made: a request body captured after its rotation can obtain no token again. It is not inert — presenting it ends that child’s live session once, which is the theft signal working as designed and the residual owner action #45 now states in full (corrected under BUG-354; the round-6 wording here said “worthless”). |

Actual: the mobile Children screen named a whole-family deletion as if it covered one child (copy fixed by the area's fixer), and the four child routes disagreed about which children a pending deletion covers: PATCH and the `deletionPending` flag tested `scope = 'family' or target_child_id = c.id`, `visibleChild` (activate, pairing-code) tested only the child scope, and archive checked nothing at all — so both clients hid Archive on a deletion-pending child the API would have archived | — | `visibleChild` now carries the same predicate the flag and PATCH use, and `POST /children/:childId/archive` goes through it instead of its own bare select, so all four child routes and both clients agree | `apps/api/tests/family-profile.review.test.ts` 'refuses archiving while the deletion is open, like the other three child routes'; failed before the change (the route answered 200 and archived the child) | Deliberately not done: `deletionPending` still carries no scope, so a client cannot say which scope a request has. That is a contract change and is left for the round that needs it |

| BUG-346 | High / COPPA §312.5 verifiable parental consent, spec P4 child privacy | closed | owner directive "put that into the system that will cover us for Parental consent" | PencilLift recorded NO per-child parental affirmation anywhere. `POST /v1/children` took nickname, gradeLevel and ageBand; `public.child_profiles` had no column naming the adult who said they are that child's parent, what they agreed to, or when. `public.consent_records` covers AI PROCESSING consent per family (provider/method/purpose/policy_version) and is a different claim from parenthood over a named child. The signup order (parent registers, pays, then hands the child a code) makes the parent FIRST in time, which is not the same as having affirmed anything about a particular child, and no verification method verifies parenthood at all — an ID-plus-selfie check establishes ADULTHOOD (a real government ID, a live face matching it), never that this adult is that child's parent. IMPACT: for every child in the product there was no record of who claimed to be their parent, so the company had nothing to produce if asked, and nothing distinguished a parent adding their own child from an adult adding someone else's. | Ruled out before filing: (a) `consent_records` is not it — its rows are per FAMILY and per PROCESSING PURPOSE, with no child column, so it cannot say who affirmed parenthood over which child; (b) parent-first ordering is not consent — verified against the spec's own AC_ACCESS_02, which orders the flow and says nothing about an affirmation; (c) the store receipt carries no age signal — the IAP verification payload was read field by field (16 fields) and holds no birth date, age band or adult flag, so "paid from an adult's Apple ID" is not evidence of adulthood the app can read; (d) a third-party VPC provider (kidentify/skills was supplied and read) would verify adulthood, not parenthood, so it does not remove the need for the affirmation. | Owner decision: confirm the adult, then take a per-child affirmation with the legal statement, then payment, then the child's code. FIX: PREMISE: the affirmation must be un-skippable and its record must be the SERVER's, so the fix works only if (i) no child row can be active without one and (ii) no client can choose the version, the instant or which adult it is recorded against. Both are observable: (i) a constraint on `public.child_profiles`, which `authenticated` reaches directly through the Data API, not only a check in the Hono handler (the BUG-240/L-037 class); (ii) the insert's values list. Migration `0970_parental_attestation.sql` adds attestation_version / attested_at / attested_by (FK auth.users) with `child_profiles_attestation_complete` (all three or none) and `child_profiles_active_requires_attestation` (`status <> 'active' or attested_at is not null`). `packages/contracts/src/family.ts` adds CONSENT_ATTESTATION_VERSION, CONSENT_ATTESTATION_STATEMENT, ATTESTATION_REQUIRED_COPY and `parentalAttestation: z.literal(true)` on the create request (a strictObject, so a client-sent version or instant is refused as an unknown key). `apps/api/src/routes/family.ts` stamps the version from the contract, the instant from `deps.clock()` and the adult from `parent.userId`. Both surfaces collect it in the SAME submission as the child's details, so no child exists un-attested even briefly, and both clear the tick after a success so it never carries to a sibling. | `supabase/tests/consent_attestation.test.ts` 9 cases: an active child with no attestation is refused; an UPDATE to 'active' is refused; an attested active child is allowed; draft and archived are left free; three all-or-none cases; the row records which adult, which version and which instant; archiving keeps the attestation. `apps/api/tests/auth.test.ts` — missing and `false` both 400 VALIDATION_FAILED, extra attestationVersion/attestedAt keys refused, and the stored version is the SERVER's constant with attested_by = the family owner. `apps/mobile/src/family/consent-attestation.test.ts` 9 cases pin the two surfaces as ONE wording and ONE behaviour. MUTATION: six, each verified red and the file restored with `sha256sum -c` — drop parentalAttestation from the phone request; drop the tick-clearing; restore the disabled button on the portal; date the attestation from the request body; reorder the insert's columns; retype the statement instead of importing it. The fourth PASSED at first: the assertion was `expect(api).toMatch(/deps\.clock\(\)/)` and other handlers in the same file call the clock (L-054), so it was re-anchored to the insert's own values list and both then went red. | The affirmation is what the product can honestly claim: it is an ATTESTATION, not verified parenthood. Adult verification (ID + live selfie) is an owner action still outstanding — `docs/Owner_Actions.md` and `docs/design/Consent_Design.md` — and until it is in place the record names a self-asserted adult. |
| BUG-347 | Medium / L-055 one rule two mechanisms, accessibility | closed | this slice, self-review before commit | My own first cut of the attestation checkbox enforced it THREE ways and got two of them wrong. (1) The submit button carried `disabled={busy !== null || !attested}`, so the `if (!attested) setFieldError(...)` branch and ATTESTATION_REQUIRED_COPY were unreachable: the parent got a dead control and no reason. (2) The checkbox's `aria-describedby={fieldError ? attestErrorId : undefined}` pointed at an id NOTHING rendered — the only error paragraph used `errorId` — so a screen reader was sent to a missing element. (3) That one paragraph sits under the Nickname field, so an attestation error would have appeared about 100px above the box it is about. IMPACT: a parent who submits without ticking gets silence on the portal; a screen-reader user gets a dangling reference; and the copy written to explain the refusal could never be shown. | Ruled out before filing: (a) not a lint or typecheck matter — both were clean with all three present, and a dangling aria-describedby is valid TSX; (b) not caught by the existing tests — the add-child cases ticked nothing and asserted nothing about the refusal path, which is why the dead branch survived review. | Root cause: I wrote the guard twice (a disabled control AND a validation branch) and let the weaker one win. FIX: one mechanism — the button stays live and the form validates on submit, reporting BOTH the nickname and the attestation in one pass so fixing one does not earn a second refusal; per-field error slots (`nicknameError`/`attestError`) each with their own rendered id, so both aria references resolve and each message sits at its own field. | `apps/web/src/pages/app/ChildrenPage.test.tsx` gained 'refuses to add a child until the parental attestation is ticked (0970)', which asserts the button is NOT disabled, that the refusal copy shows, that `sends` is empty, and that the id in `aria-describedby` resolves to an element whose text is the refusal — the assertion the dangling id would fail. | BUG-348 | High / spec P4 deletion stops processing, AC_PRIVACY export withdrawal | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-D-1/D-4, fixed and corrected by the lead in this slice | An export could be PUBLISHED after the deletion that was meant to stop it. `childrenBeingDeleted` runs inside the build transaction under READ COMMITTED and BEFORE the file is composed, so a deletion committing after that SELECT left the excluded-children list empty and the deleted child's child_profiles, assignments, extracted_questions (prompt_text, student_answer_text, corrected_*), question_results, attempts, points_ledger, child_feedback and safety_reports rows all went into the file; and for a FAMILY-scope request that check sees nothing at all, because it reads `target_child_id`. The settle's only guard was `status = 'queued'` — the status 0920/0930 had deliberately left standing on the false justification "the builder leaves out every child with an open deletion request (privacy.ts)", repeated in three places — so the row flipped to 'ready' with a live storage_path and a seven-day expiry AFTER the request, and `routes/export-download.ts` (which reads kind, status, storage_path and expires_at and nothing about deletion) minted a signed URL for it. On a child-scope request the family is live, the membership is active and `assertRecentUnlock` is satisfied by the very unlock the deletion required. IMPACT: the parent who asked PencilLift to delete their child's data could download that child's homework for seven days; the window closed only when deletion_purge ran, unbounded if that job dead-lettered. HUNT7-D-4: `app.inactivity_delete_family`, the other writer of a family-scope request, had no `public.data_exports` statement at all, so both residuals lived on that path too. | Ruled out before filing: (a) the job-cancellation statement is not what stops it — a family-wide export_build job carries child_id NULL, so on a child-scope request `child_id = p_child` is NULL and the job is not cancelled, and a job already leased is 'running' and is not cancelled on either scope; (b) the handler's `join public.families f on f.deleted_at is null` covers family scope only and is taken once before the build; (c) the download route resolves nothing about deletion. | Lead: CONFIRMED — and the first fix was too blunt, which is the part worth recording. It made the settle refuse on ANY open deletion request, which reddened a pre-existing spec-P4 case (`tests/export-build.test.ts`: on a child-scope deletion the family-wide file must stay deliverable, carrying the sibling and not the deleted child) — i.e. the fix destroyed an export the family is entitled to, for every parent who had one in flight, and the migration carried the same blunt arm (`or child_id is null`). FIX, corrected: PREMISE: what makes a queued row dangerous is not that a deletion is open but that the BYTES were composed without honouring it, so the only precise test compares the requests open at publish time against the exclusion set the file was actually composed from. That set is observable if it is recorded: `build()` now reads `childrenBeingDeleted` ONCE before any section is composed (it was read separately by `progressRows` and by `familyData`, so two sections of one family_data file could honour different sets) and returns it on `Built.excludedChildren`. The settle refuses when a FAMILY-scope request is open, or a CHILD-scope one whose target is absent from that snapshot; a request the snapshot already honours publishes. Migration 0960 keeps the half it can judge without reading a file — every queued row on a family deletion, the deleted child's own queued exports on a child deletion — and deliberately LEAVES a family-wide queued row for the builder, with the division of labour written into both files. The asymmetry with the 'ready' statement above it (which does take `or child_id is null`) is stated too: a finished file was composed before the request and certainly holds the child's rows. `app.inactivity_delete_family` gets both statements at the caller's STATED clock (p_now), not `now()` — a date comparison on an unstated clock is a defect this project has paid for twice (BUG-090, and this round's job-retention step). The dispatcher's late-removal list is keyed on `status !== 'ready'`, so the row 0960 settles to 'failed' keeps its `private.storage_removals` entry. | `supabase/tests/hardening_r5_db.test.ts` runs the builder's own settle statement as the job would run it a moment after the deletion committed, and asserts it returns no row for the deleted child's export while the family-wide row stays 'queued' for the builder. `apps/api/tests/privacy-r2.review.test.ts` covers both halves: the race (compose, deletion, settle → refused, 409 on download, uploaded bytes removed) and the division of labour (the child's own row 'failed' by 0960, the family-wide row left 'queued', and the purge scheduling a late storage pass for BOTH — the 'failed' one is exactly what `status === 'queued'` would have missed). `tests/export-build.test.ts`'s spec-P4 case passes unchanged, which is the evidence the correction needed. MUTATION: four, each red and each file restored with `sha256sum -c` — drop the settle's deletion check (the original defect); re-read the deletion set at settle instead of carrying the snapshot (the READ COMMITTED race — 8 cases red); give 0960 the broad arm back; key the late-removal list on `status === 'queued'`. | The family-scope arm of the 'ready' withdrawal is kept as defence in depth, not because a tombstoned family's adult can reach the download — `export-download.ts` resolves `currentFamilyId` from an ACTIVE membership before it reads `public.data_exports`, and both family-scope writers revoke those memberships in the same transaction. 0920/0930's claim that the arm was load-bearing is corrected in those files. |
| BUG-349 | Low / CLAUDE.md "child writes go through API-owned SECURITY DEFINER functions" | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-D-3 | The `pl_child` write invariant's column arm had an unplanted privilege: the column-level INSERT its own lead example writes out in full (`grant insert (family_id, child_id) on public.<family table> to pl_child`). The query has four independent lists — two grantee lists and two privilege lists — and round 6 pinned all four GRANTEE cells with five plants, then said in the comment that "no arm can be dropped". Both column-level plants were UPDATE, so `'INSERT'` could be deleted from the column arm's privilege list and all five plants stayed green: plant 1 survives because the TABLE arm still names PUBLIC and INSERT, and `information_schema.role_column_grants` expands a table-level grant to every column. IMPACT: no live hole — the invariant does catch such a grant today — but the one arm a future narrowing edit could delete with CI green was the arm protecting family tables from a paired child's direct INSERT. HUNT6-E-4 with one word moved: there the deletable word was a grantee, here a privilege. | Ruled out before filing: the hunter verified all four grantee mutations still red (dropping 'PUBLIC' from the table arm reds plant 2, 'pl_child' from it reds plant 5, 'PUBLIC' from the column arm reds plant 4, 'pl_child' from it reds plant 3), so this is not a re-file of E-4; and checked TRUNCATE, which also has no plant here, is covered by the '[DB-R2-04]' case. | Lead: CONFIRMED. Plant it, so the arm can fail. FIX: plant 6 is `grant insert (metadata) on public.audit_events to pl_child`, a column-level INSERT no table-level view reports, so only the column arm's `'INSERT'` can see it. AND — found while proving the fix — the TABLE arm's `'INSERT'` and `'UPDATE'` can BOTH be deleted with every plant green, which is redundancy rather than a hole: the column view expands a table-level INSERT/UPDATE to every column, so the column arm reports those grants too, and plant 1 (a table-level insert) surviving that deletion is the demonstration. The words only the table arm can report are DELETE and TRUNCATE, and both are planted. That is written into the comment, so every word in the query is now either pinned by a plant or provably redundant and the next reader who mutates one knows which. | `supabase/tests/schema_invariants.test.ts` 10 cases. MUTATION: deleting `'INSERT'` from the column arm reds plant 6 and nothing else (verified; restored with `sha256sum -c`); deleting it from the table arm stays green, which is the redundancy now documented rather than a second finding. | The redundancy is left in place: the table arm's privilege list reads as the complete set of write privileges, which is what a reader checks it against, and narrowing it to DELETE/TRUNCATE would make the query harder to read for no gain. |
| BUG-350 | Low / L-053 when code and prose disagree, decide which is TRUE | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-D-2 | Migration 0930 defines "the canonical order" for this codebase and then claimed the archive route, the billing writers and the purges "now agree with this function statement for statement". False in three places: `public.request_deletion` revokes `child_sessions` and `child_devices` BEFORE the {child_slot_assignments, child_profiles} pair while the archive route writes them AFTER it, so for {child_profiles, child_sessions} and {child_slot_assignments, child_sessions} the two are inverted; `app.purge_family_data` deletes the assignment at a different point; and `public.data_exports` is not written by the archive route at all. IMPACT: no deadlock is reachable, because every one of these writers serialises on the family row first — but 0930 is the file the next author reads, and it told them the sequences agree, so they would conclude that copying this function's order is what keeps a new writer safe. It is not. | Ruled out before filing: the hunter enumerated every writer of `child_profiles` and `child_slot_assignments` (activate, archive, PATCH profile, create child, the pairing-code route, billing's applyCapacity/releaseSlotlessProfiles under all three FOR UPDATE callers, `app.purge_family_data`, `app.inactivity_delete_family`, `app.enforce_slot_capacity`) to confirm the SECOND clause — no cycle among the pair's writers — is sound, so only the first clause is disputed. | Lead: CONFIRMED as prose. Say which differ and why it is safe; do not leave the claim standing. FIX: 0930's comment now leads with the claim that carries the weight — every writer of this pair takes the family row first, by FOR UPDATE or by the KEY SHARE the `deletion_requests` foreign key needs — keeps the agreement that is true (assignment, then profile) and names the three disagreements explicitly, with the conclusion a reader needs: a new writer takes the family row first or re-derives this argument from scratch. My first draft of the correction cited four line numbers and three were already stale (my own edit had shifted 0930's), so it cites STATEMENTS instead and says why. | `supabase/tests/hardening_r5_db.test.ts` pins the claim in BOTH directions, because a comment asserting a disagreement can rot the other way: the function's order is read from `pg_get_functiondef` and asserted sessions → devices → assignment → profile; the archive route's is read from source and asserted assignment → profile → sessions → devices; and 0930 is asserted to carry the corrected text and not the false sentence. The family-row-first claim itself was already proven by the real interleaving case in the same file. MUTATION: two, both red and both restored with `sha256sum -c` — put 0930's false sentence back; reorder the archive route to write sessions before the pair. | — |
| BUG-351 | Medium / spend control, owner cost visibility | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-B-2 | `docs/Cost_Analysis.md`'s worst-case-spend-HOLD table omitted the Thursday review's personalization hold, and the paragraph under it named 483,240 micro-USD ($0.48) as "the largest single hold a family can hit". The largest is 933,600 ($0.93), 93% more. Not a hypothetical path: `personalizeItems` takes its hold as `acquireSpendHold(deps, PROPOSED_STAGE_COST_BUDGET_MICROS[stage])` with `stage: 'daily_set' | 'thursday_bundle'`; `generateThursdayReview` is registered as the `thursday_review_generate` handler, is enqueued by the scheduled tick, and calls `personalizeItems` with `'thursday_bundle'`; and `PROPOSED_STAGE_COST_BUDGET_MICROS.thursday_bundle` is 933,600. IMPACT: the document the owner reads to decide what PencilLift may spend understated its biggest single reservation by almost half, so a spend ceiling set from that table would have been set too low and the Thursday job would have been refused a hold it needs. | Ruled out before filing: the figure is present in the STAGE table two tables up (`| thursday_bundle | 700,000 | 933,600 | 933,600 |`), so this is a hold-table omission and a false sentence, not a wrong constant — the code is right and the prose is wrong (L-053). | Lead: CONFIRMED. MUTATION: five, each restored with `sha256sum -c` — delete the Thursday hold row; restore the understated sentence; change `thursday_bundle`'s budget without touching the doc; change coaching's admission cap without touching the doc; delete a stage-table row. THE FIRST ONE PASSED AT FIRST, which is the part worth recording: the hold-table search was bounded to "any table line containing the stage name and the figure", and after deleting the hold row the STAGE table's own row satisfied it — the defect the test exists to catch, passing. The search is now bounded to the one table with the header `| Hold | Stages summed | Micro-USD | USD |` and all five mutations red (L-054). | The two tables stay hand-written and are checked against the code rather than generated into the document: the prose around them explains WHY each number is what it is, which a generator cannot write. |
| BUG-352 | Medium / MOB-R2-05 / BUG-287 (the child's own space must allow capture) / L-051 cached state has an owner | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-A-1; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | Round 6 moved `setScreenPrivacy(false)` out of `lockParentAreaOnDevice` and replaced it with a module-level debt flag (`childSpaceOwedScreenPrivacyOff` in src/lib/mode.ts) for `settleChildSpaceScreenPrivacy` to apply later. The backgrounding handler in src/lib/app-session.ts reaches that assignment only after three awaited SecureStore round trips — `readMode()`, then inside the lock the `getItem(childRefreshToken)` read and the `setItem(mode,'child')` write — while the same handler's 'active' branch is synchronous and returns at once. So on a quick background/foreground cycle (the app switcher, the notification shade, a dismissed call, an Android onPause/onResume pair) 'active' fires while that chain is still pending, `settleChildSpaceScreenPrivacy` reads the flag as false and does nothing, and the chain then sets it true. The app is now foregrounded, mode is 'child', `resetNavigationToChildHome()` has run, and OS capture protection is still ON from `enterParentMode`; `useChildModeOnFocus` returns early because the mode it reads is already 'child', and the Lock button's own settle never runs on this path. Before the change the lock applied `setScreenPrivacy(false)` itself, so the outcome did not depend on event ordering. IMPACT: a child is handed back a tablet whose own space silently refuses screenshots, screen recording and casting until the app is backgrounded and foregrounded again — and on Android FLAG_SECURE also blackens a cast of the whole screen, so a parent mirroring their child's homework to a TV gets a black frame with no explanation. | Ruled out before filing: (a) that some other path re-applies it — every `setScreenPrivacy` caller was enumerated (the settle, `signOutParent`, `enterParentMode`, `enterChildMode`, the runtime effect) and none runs here, since the mode is already 'child' so `useChildModeOnFocus`/`enterChildMode` return early and no sign-out or parent-mode entry happens; (b) that the flag might be set before the event — it is assigned strictly after three awaits, so any 'active' delivered inside that window loses the race, and that is the ORDINARY interleaving for a sub-100 ms foreground return, not a contrived one; (c) that a killed process makes it moot — a different case, already covered, since a fresh process starts with capture allowed; (d) that a test already covered it — the round-6 case in app-session.test.ts always does `await settle()` after 'background' before firing 'active', so it cannot see this ordering; (e) that 'active' might not fire on a short cycle — it is the only foreground event React Native delivers on Android, and on iOS the sequence is 'inactive' then 'active', so it still arrives. | Lead: CONFIRMED. A module-level debt flag settled by an event that may already have passed is L-051 plus a race; settle it where the caller knows the app is foreground, or re-check after recording the debt. FIX: PREMISE: the only fact `AppState` can state at the end of the lock is whether the app is in the foreground AT THAT MOMENT, so the settle is offered there as well instead of being left to an event that may have gone by — a device still on its way out leaves the adult frame protected, which is HUNT6-I-4 unchanged. `lockParentAreaOnDevice` now RETURNS whether a debt is still owed (`return childSpaceOwesScreenPrivacy()`, after the relock, so a settle that overtook the lock is not double-counted), and src/lib/app-session.ts's backgrounding handler settles it when `AppState.currentState === 'active'` once the lock has finished. | apps/mobile/src/lib/app-session.test.ts > 'leaving the app locks the parent area on the device too (MOB-R2-01)' > '[repro] a foreground return inside the lock still gets the child space its screen privacy': 'background' and 'active' are fired back to back with no `await settle()` between them, then `fake.privacy` must be `[false]` and `childSpaceOwesScreenPrivacy()` false. The react-native mock in that file was changed so a change event moves `AppState.currentState` to the new value BEFORE the listener runs, as the OS does, and its `afterEach` drains the debt with a no-op effect so cases stay independent. MUTATION: `0d8fdf4`'s message records that every fix in it was shown red first and mutation-checked with the production line reverted and each file restored byte-for-byte with `sha256sum -c`; no mutation specific to this finding is named. | — |
| BUG-353 | Medium / MOB-R2-01 (the adult frame is protected from the app switcher and from capture) / HUNT6-I-4 | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-A-2; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | `settleChildSpaceScreenPrivacy` applied `setScreenPrivacy(false)` on the sole condition that the debt flag was set: it did not read the mode, and nothing cleared the flag — `enterParentMode` writes mode 'parent' and `setScreenPrivacy(true)` and left it standing, as did `enterChildMode` and `signOutParent`. The session layer calls the settle on EVERY 'active' transition whatever the mode, and on iOS 'active' arrives after any 'inactive' blip that never reached 'background' — control centre, the notification shade, a Face ID or permission dialog, a dismissed call — none of which runs the lock. Two ways to hold a stale debt: HUNT7-A-1's ordering race, and the Lock button's own chain, which records the debt and settles only after `await effects.relockOnServer()`, an authenticated POST on the client's default timeout, so a parent who PINs back in first gets `setScreenPrivacy(false)` delivered into parent mode. The harm then composes with the leak HUNT6-I-4 fixed: with protection off in parent mode the next backgrounding runs the lock, which no longer turns protection on, and the OS snapshots the still-painted ADULT frame for its app-switcher card, because `resetNavigationToChildHome` is a JS `router.replace` and cannot paint while the app is suspended. IMPACT: whoever picks the tablet up next — in a family product that is the child — can screenshot, screen-record or cast the parent area, and sees the adult frame on the app-switcher card: the family name, the children's nicknames and grades, the planner and the billing screen. | Ruled out before filing: (a) that some caller clears the debt on leaving the child space — every writer of the flag was read (one set, one clear in the settle, nothing else assigns it) and `enterParentMode` does not; (b) that the settle is harmless in parent mode because something re-asserts protection — the only `setScreenPrivacy(true)` is in `enterParentMode`, which has already run, and no focus effect or screen re-applies it; (c) that 'active' only ever follows 'background', which would mean the lock always ran first — React Native delivers 'inactive' → 'active' on iOS for non-backgrounding interruptions and the handler matched 'active' with no guard on what preceded it; (d) the Lock-button path is not rested on alone, which is why the ordering race is named first; (e) that an existing test covers it — the mode-r2 case settles immediately after the lock and its `afterEach` drains the flag between cases, so no case ever held a debt across an `enterParentMode`. | Lead: CONFIRMED and worse than A-1: an unscoped debt turns ADULT screen privacy off while the parent area is on screen. Make the debt name what it is owed for and re-read the mode at settle time. FIX: PREMISE: a deferred change is safe only if it names the state it was computed for and re-checks that state before applying anything, and only the keychain can say which space the device is in. The bare boolean became `screenPrivacyDebt: { space: 'child'; storage: SecureStorage } \| null`, so the debt carries the store it was recorded against rather than trusting whatever store a later caller happens to hold; `settleChildSpaceScreenPrivacy` re-reads `currentMode(debt.storage)` and applies nothing unless the mode is still the debt's space, failing closed when that read rejects (a mode that cannot be read is not the child's space); and the three paths that apply a privacy state of their own — `enterParentMode`, `enterChildMode`, `signOutParent` — call `forgetScreenPrivacyDebt()`. The debt is still dropped before anything is awaited, so two settles cannot both apply it and an OS that refuses is not retried on every foreground. | apps/mobile/src/lib/mode-r2.review.test.ts, three cases in 'locking a paired family tablet returns it to the child space (MOB-R4-LOCK-01)': '[repro] a debt that outlives the return to parent mode does not strip adult protection' (lock, then `enterParentMode`, then the settle must answer false and never call `privacy:false`); '[repro] a settle re-reads the mode, so a debt is never applied outside the child space', which writes the mode straight into the store to stand in for any OTHER path out of the child space, because one caller remembering to drop the debt is not the property; and 'a keychain that cannot say which space the device is in keeps adult protection on'. MUTATION: none specific to this finding is recorded; `0d8fdf4` records the round-wide mutation check with `sha256sum -c`. | — |
| BUG-354 | **Medium** / spec P3 / docs/Threat_Model.md T20 / L-058 and BUG-294 (a recorded residual may not be smaller than the real one) — filed against a claim the lead wrote in round 6 | closed (residual in Next) | round-7 hunt HUNT7-A-3, against the lead's own round-6 wording at `5ba16c3`; code and test fixed in `0d8fdf4` | Presenting a rotated child refresh token is not inert, it is an ACTION. `POST /v1/child/refresh`'s `row.used_at` branch unconditionally revokes the child's live session (`update public.child_sessions set revoked_at = …, revoke_reason = 'refresh_token_reuse'`), inserts an `audit_events` row, and revokes the device row through `stampDeviceWhenNoLiveSession` so the parent's list stops calling the tablet connected. Nothing authenticates the presenter, and nothing bounds it but `childRefreshPerNetwork` (240/hour) — the per-session rule is reached only on the success path, so a replayer never spends it. Migration 0850 keeps every token of a LIVE session however old and prunes only `SESSION_ROW_RETENTION_DAYS` (30) after the session ends, so the window is the whole life of the session, not a moment; and the audit insert had no guard, so each further replay appended another row to a table nothing prunes. Five places asserted the opposite: the route's own comment ("a captured body is worthless again ONCE THE TOKEN IN IT HAS BEEN ROTATED"), the mobile-r2 docstring, BUG-244's ledger row, docs/Requirement_Coverage.md and owner action #45. The tree contradicted the claim in its own threat model, T20 being titled "Child session revoked by token replay". IMPACT: an attacker holding one captured refresh body can unpair a named child's tablet on demand for the life of that session plus the 30-day token retention — the child is told to ask a grown-up, and the parent completes a PIN step-up, mints a new pairing code and re-enters its 8 symbols before the child can work again — and the owner was being asked in owner action #45 to accept BUG-244 on a basis that is wrong in the direction that makes the residual look SMALLER. | Ruled out before filing: (1) the capability is not new — `git diff 1cfebcc 5ba16c3 -- apps/api/src/routes/child-auth.ts` shows the `row.used_at` body byte-identical to pre-BUG-244, comments only, so the CLAIM is filed and not a code regression; (2) the row is not gone by replay time — migration 0850 keeps every token of a live session, so it is not a no-op 401; (3) the revoke does commit on a 401 — the branch returns `{ kind: 'reused' }` rather than throwing, so `deps.db.asService` commits before the route raises UNAUTHENTICATED; (4) a rate limit does not make it a single shot — only the per-network rule runs before the body is parsed; (5) "buys no ACCESS" is not a defensible reading of "worthless" and "buy an attacker nothing", stated unqualified and offered to the owner as "the property a logged request body needs"; (6) the capture premise is not supported in-tree either, which makes the over-claim worse rather than better — this API logs event/requestId/status/durationMs only, so the vector is a proxy or middlebox in front of it; (7) no parent is told the false thing directly, since no route or screen reads `revoke_reason` or `audit_events`, which is why it was not filed high. | Lead: CONFIRMED, AND THIS ONE IS MINE. The claim I wrote in five places — code comment, BUG-244's row, owner action #45, Requirement_Coverage and the round-6 commit message — is false: presenting a rotated token is an ACTION, not an inert no-op, so a captured body is a remote unpair for that child's tablet, and I recorded the residual SMALLER than it is in the record the owner signs off from. That is precisely the failure BUG-294 and L-058 exist to correct, committed inside the change that corrected it. FIX THE PROSE, KEEP THE CODE: PREMISE: the revoke is correct and is the theft signal, so what must change is what is claimed about it. The route comment now states exactly what a captured body can and cannot do — it can no longer OBTAIN TOKENS, which is the only property the removal takes back; its FIRST presentation ends that child's live session and un-lists the device, so one captured body is one unpairing at an instant its holder chooses while that session lives; and no later presentation ends it again. AND the one code change the lead accepted: the update is captured as `const revoked = await tx\`update public.child_sessions …\`` and the audit insert runs only `if (revoked.count > 0)`, so a replay loop cannot grow a table nothing prunes — a bound on an attacker's write amplification, not a weakening of detection, with the trade stated in the comment (a rotated token presented against a session some other path already ended now records nothing, and is refused just the same). | apps/api/tests/mobile-r2.review.test.ts, in 'the refresh request carries no recovery id at all (BUG-244 reopened, HUNT6-A-1)': '[repro] a captured body obtains no token after its rotation, and still ends the session' — written first AS the round-6 claim (`expect(…revoke_reason).toBeNull()`), where it failed with "expected 'refresh_token_reuse' to be null", then asserted the other way up, replaying from a different `cf-connecting-ip` so the presenter is not the tablet: 401, no `refreshToken` property, `revoke_reason` 'refresh_token_reuse', device `revokedAt` not null. And 'replaying a spent body again revokes nothing more and adds no second audit row', whose own comment records the mutation the guard answers: unguarded, the four refusals wrote four rows. The file's docstring is rewritten into the three parts those cases pin. | CLOSED. All five records now say what is true. docs/Owner_Actions.md #45, docs/Requirement_Coverage.md AC_ACCESS_04, BUG-244’s row above and BUG-294’s row above each replace “worthless” with the narrower property the removal really buys — the body obtains no token — and each states the residual it does NOT buy: the first presentation still ends that child’s live session and un-lists the tablet. docs/Threat_Model.md T20 now names the external replayer as a separate actor from the device, with the window (the life of the session plus the 30-day retention), the only bound that is reached (childRefreshPerNetwork, 240/hour), the four partial mitigations and, explicitly, what is not mitigated. BUG-294’s row is annotated rather than rewritten, so the record of what was believed in round 6 survives beside the correction. Nothing about the code changed: the revoke is the theft signal and is correct — this was a prose defect, and per L-053 the prose was what was wrong. The lesson that applies is BUG-294’s own (a recorded residual may not be smaller than the real one), which is why this row exists at all: the lead committed it inside the change that corrected it. Previous text of this cell: “the four records still carry the false claim” |
| BUG-355 | Low / L-053 when code and prose disagree, decide which is TRUE; round-6's own rule that a fix states its premise and the observable fact establishing it — filed against a claim the lead wrote in round 6 | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-A-4; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | The guard is `if (row.used_at)`, and its comment inferred "Rotation is the only way a token becomes used, so a second presentation means two parties hold it". What the guard establishes is the ADJACENT fact — this token has been rotated before. One party presenting the same token twice satisfies it exactly: that is BUG-244, and the same comment conceded it three lines down ("this also fires when a rotation's response is lost on the way back to the tablet"). The counterexample is a green test in the tree with one party and no attacker — apps/mobile/src/family/child-session-r2.review.test.ts > '[repro] a retry after a lost answer presents the spent token, and the tablet is unpaired' — whose mock server rotates and then drops the answer. The second sentence, "the feature only avoided an occasional unpairing a parent can undo in one tap", is wrong on the product: minting a new pairing code needs `assertRecentUnlock` on POST /children/:childId/pairing-code, a parent-area navigation, the code request, then 'Use a different code' and an 8-symbol entry on the tablet, which is what the same comment says two paragraphs up. IMPACT: no user-visible effect. This comment is the only statement of the rule at the point of enforcement, and it told the next reviewer the guard PROVES theft — the belief that made BUG-244 ship — while understating the cost of the accepted defect in the same paragraph that justifies accepting it. | Ruled out before filing: (1) that the sentence is a fair shorthand — it is stated as an inference ("so"), and the branch's whole difficulty is that the inference does not hold, which the next paragraph concedes; L-053 applies with the CODE as the true side, since revoking on any re-presentation is the right rule; (2) that 'in one tap' refers to something else, e.g. a device-list action — the parent surfaces offer 'Disconnect', not 'reconnect', there is no one-tap re-pair, and the pairing-code route requires a fresh unlock; (3) that the removal itself is wrong — it is not, and no behaviour change is asked for; (4) that another file states it correctly and so this is harmless — apps/api/tests/auth.test.ts and packages/contracts/src/auth.ts both state the rule without the false inference, which is why the disagreement is worth resolving rather than leaving two records. | Lead: CONFIRMED as prose. Say what the guard establishes rather than what would be nice: a second presentation means the token was presented twice, and the code three lines down already states the race honestly; make the two sentences agree. FIX: the comment now leads with "This token has already been rotated, so it must never be served again", states that the rotation below is the one thing that sets `used_at` so a second presentation means the token was presented twice, and says explicitly that it does NOT establish that two parties hold it — the second presenter is either a second holder or the rightful holder retrying after a response that never arrived, the server cannot tell them apart, so it fails closed and treats it as theft — citing the one-party green test by name. The BUG-244 paragraph now spells out the re-pairing cost (PIN step-up, a new pairing code minted through `assertRecentUnlock`, its 8 symbols re-entered) and ends "It is not an unpairing anyone can undo in one tap; no surface offers a re-pair action at all." | Prose in a comment, and the finding says plainly that no assertion can make the sentence true: the counterexample is the ALREADY-GREEN apps/mobile/src/family/child-session-r2.review.test.ts > '[repro] a retry after a lost answer presents the spent token, and the tablet is unpaired', which passes today with exactly one party presenting the token twice, and which the corrected comment now cites by name. No test in `0d8fdf4` pins this wording and none is claimed; the regression evidence for the wording itself is not identifiable from the diff. | — |
| BUG-356 | Medium / owner cost visibility and spend control / L-053 | closed (residual in Next) | round-7 hunt HUNT7-B-1; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | `runStage` picks the cost bound on `attempt === 1` ALONE — `canAttempt(attempt === 1 ? limits : { ...limits, maxCostMicros: maxStageCostMicros }, …)` — with no `budgetRaised` condition, so the stage BUDGET governs the cumulative-cost check for every attempt after the first, whatever ended the earlier one: a truncation, a client-side timeout, a 5xx or a validation rejection alike (every branch that reaches `attempt += 1` continues with `maxOutputTokens` unchanged). Coaching's floor input is 1,626 tokens and its production envelope adds the printed question and the child's answer, so ordinary coaching requests sit inside the newly opened band. Meanwhile `PROPOSED_STAGE_COST_BUDGET_MICROS`'s own docstring said "These are HOLDS, not spend: settleSpend replaces each with the actual usage", and docs/Cost_Analysis.md section 3 repeated it. IMPACT: the owner pays for one extra astra generation on every transient-failure path in that band — up to about $0.20 for coaching, $0.24 for daily_set, $0.47 for thursday_bundle — while the two documents they would read to understand the round-6 change told them the change moves no money. | Ruled out before filing: (a) not a ceiling breach — the hold reserves the same budget at both hold sites and the cumulative check bounds metered spend by it, with the group sum checked by hand (grading 216,816 + verification 100,000 = 316,816, exactly what `spending(['grading','verification'])` reserves); (b) not pre-existing — at `b802686` coaching/followup/daily_set/thursday_bundle/semantic_check carried identical caps and the pre-diff raise and loop both used `limits.maxCostMicros`, so the whole stage was bounded by 300,000, and the round-6 diff changed no limit VALUE, only added the budget table; (c) not confined to truncation — every `attempt += 1` branch was traced and all are weighed against the budget; (d) not caught by a test — the existing "timed-out attempts count against the stage budget" case uses extraction, whose two numbers are the same 216,816, so it cannot observe the widening, and no case drove an astra stage with a 5xx; (e) `run.ts`'s own loop comment already said "Every LATER attempt is weighed against the budget", so the loop is accurate and the finding is against the constant's docstring and the doc. | Lead: CONFIRMED. Decide which is true (L-053), and I decide the CODE is right and the PROSE is wrong: the budget SHOULD bound every later attempt cumulatively, because that is what a spend hold reserves and what the owner is quoted. Do NOT narrow the code to the truncation retry only — that would make the hold larger than what it bounds, which is the defect just fixed from the other direction. FIX: `PROPOSED_STAGE_COST_BUDGET_MICROS`'s docstring now separates the TWO things weighed against the number, saying only one settles back: (1) the spend HOLD, which settleSpend replaces with metered usage, so a stage that SUCCEEDS costs no more than before the split; (2) the CUMULATIVE metered cost of every attempt after the first, which is real spend and, for the five stages whose budget is above their cap, a LARGER bound than the single number that governed the whole stage before. The table is not hand-maintained: `packages/ai/src/cost-doc.test.ts` > 'the cost record carries the second-billed-attempt bands' asserts it against the same figures in routing.ts’s docstring, and derives the expected STAGE LIST from the constants (budget above cap, maxAttempts ≥ 2) rather than writing it down, so it cannot pass on an empty list. run-metering.test.ts already pinned docstring ↔ computed bands, so the chain document ↔ docstring ↔ computation has every link asserted (L-066, named in both files). Nothing enforced that declaration afterwards — `runOne` awaits the handler with no deadline, and scan-process.ts had no wall-clock check at all, its only early exit being `pause()` at the spend ceiling. THE CHECKER GRADED THE FIRST FIX'S PREMISE FALSE and found it made the product WORSE than the defect it closed: the same gate was applied to the `rubric` step, which has no fallback, so a written-work question lost its labels entirely while the assignment was still delivered `ready` — telling a parent the work was checked when part of it was not, with a warn log the only trace — and it was gated on TEN TIMES its own worst case (coaching's 101,500 ms against one moderation call's 10,000 ms), refusing labels with up to 91,500 ms of budget nothing else could spend. The rubric step now has its own bound, and when even that does not fit the scan goes to parent review instead of reporting `ready`: a grown-up is told to look rather than a child quietly getting less. | apps/api/tests/jobs-r2.review.test.ts > 'the tick claim budget (JOBS-R2-07)' > 'declares a scan worst case its own stage timeouts and attempt counts justify (HUNT7-B-3)' derives the sum from the production constants and asserts it is within `SCAN_AI_WALL_BUDGET_MS`. apps/api/tests/scan-process.test.ts covers the enforcement and the checker's three findings: 'stops calling the tutor once the wall time the job was claimed with is gone (HUNT7-B-3)', 'rubric labels are gated on ONE moderation call, not on coaching's worst case (HUNT7-B-3)' and 'a written-work question the wall budget cannot label sends the scan to a grown-up, never `ready` (HUNT7-B-3)'. | `SCAN_AI_WALL_BUDGET_MS` bounds the AI and moderation work only. | BUG-359 | Low (lead: "it matters more than low") / BUG-260 and BUG-309 (a promised retry the stage cannot pay for); spec P12 versioned billing rates | closed (residual in Next) | round-7 hunt HUNT7-B-5; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | `fullRaiseCeiling` priced both its terms against `DEFAULT_RATE_TABLE_2026_09_18` literally, and `defineStageCostBudgets` runs once at module import — while `runStage` prices the same two terms against `options.rates`, which every caller supplies (`this.rates` in scan-process.ts, `options.rates ?? DEFAULT_RATE_TABLE_2026_09_18` in learning-jobs.ts). Rates are explicitly versioned and expected to change (packages/domain/src/quotas/rates.ts, and spec P12 "Keep billing rates in a versioned server table"), and `AttemptRecord.rateTableVersion` exists precisely so a second version can be introduced. On the day a newer default table is added and wired, the startup check would still validate the budgets against the September table and pass, while `raisedOutputBudget` priced the retry at the new rates and returned a shrunken budget or null — the BUG-260/BUG-309 failure, silently, with the guard that exists to prevent it green. The docstring's enumeration of what fails the worker at startup (a new stage, a raised `maxOutputTokens`, a lowered number) omitted the rate table, which is the only one of the four inputs not held in that file. IMPACT: not reachable today — no second rate table exists and no production caller passes one — so nothing a family or the owner meets. FIX: BOTH. `fullRaiseCeiling(stage, maxOutputTokens, rates = DEFAULT_RATE_TABLE_2026_09_18)` takes the table as an argument and prices `computeOperationCostMicros` and `estimateUpperBoundCostMicros` at it; `defineStageCostBudgets`' docstring now says it validates the recorded budgets AT THAT rate table and names the rate table as the one input it cannot see; and `runStage` fails closed for any other table — when `rates !== DEFAULT_RATE_TABLE_2026_09_18` and the stage retries, it re-derives the ceiling at those rates and throws a `RangeError` naming the stage, the budget, the ceiling and `rates.version` if the budget is below it. A table equal to the default by value is re-derived too and passes, so only a real price change is refused, and a table that cannot price the stage's model at all is left to the loop's own UNKNOWN_MODEL result, with no spend. | packages/ai/src/run-truncation.test.ts > 'the full-raise ceiling is priced against the rates it is judged against (HUNT7-B-5)', four cases: 'prices the ceiling at the table it is given, not at the September one', 'refuses the stage when the caller's rates put the raise past the budget', 'runs a caller whose rates the recorded budget still affords', and 'routing.ts names the rate table among what fails the worker at startup'.
