The moment it is made, these cases build a ceiling of 150,000 while the hold asks for 216,816, so extraction is refused at `acquireSpendHold` and the cases fail while asserting the opposite of what they mean. A sibling case also still named grading's pre-HUNT5-C-1 figure of 150,000 micros. IMPACT: no user outcome — the cases are green and their assertions currently correct. The cost is a fixer's next round: the owner change routing.ts advertises turns three unrelated scan cases red for a reason none of them names, and the premise the cases state in prose ("room for exactly one stage") is no longer the number they compute. | Ruled out before filing: (a) the numbers really do coincide today, so this is latent rather than a live failure — extraction's and grading's budgets are `EXTRACTION_GRADING_COST_MICROS` and verification's is 100,000, each equal to its cap, and the LJA-F4 sum 533,632 equals the two holds 216,816 + 316,816; (b) the production side WAS updated — `spending()` sums the budget table and `personalizeItems` reserves it, so the gap was only in these test call sites; (c) apps/api/tests/spend-ceiling.test.ts is unaffected, using bare literals against `acquireSpendHold` and referencing neither table; (d) the mutation is legal rather than a straw man, since cap 150,000 with budget 216,816 passes both guards. | Lead: CONFIRMED. Three spend-ceiling cases still size the owner's budget from the ADMISSION caps after production moved its holds to the budgets, so they are green for the wrong reason. Re-derive them from `PROPOSED_STAGE_COST_BUDGET_MICROS`. FIX: all three now build the ceiling from `PROPOSED_STAGE_COST_BUDGET_MICROS` — extraction alone in the two single-stage cases, and `extraction + grading + verification` in LJA-F4 — with their comments saying the reservation is the stage BUDGET, matching the wording the two learning cases already had; and the sibling case's stale figure now names verification's 100,000 with a note that the line had carried grading's pre-HUNT5-C-1 150,000. | apps/api/tests/scan-process.test.ts, the three cases re-derived in place: 'the spend ceiling pauses a scan without spending an attempt…', LJA-F4 and LJA-F5, each importing `PROPOSED_STAGE_COST_BUDGET_MICROS` from `@pencillift/ai` and computing the `spend_budgets` row from it. MUTATION: the finding's reproduction is the mutation the fix makes visible — giving extraction and grading `maxCostMicros: 150_000` while leaving the budgets at 216,816, the one-line owner change routing.ts advertises. No mutation specific to this finding is recorded in `0d8fdf4` beyond its round-wide `sha256sum -c` check. | — |
| BUG-363 | Medium / BILL-R1-1 (clawed-back money must never read as kept revenue) / C-PRORATION-REVERSAL | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-C-1; fixed with HUNT7-C-2 as one change in `0d8fdf4` | `applySettlementToPeriod` computed `leftoverCents = max(0, refundedCents - current.charged_amount_cents - tax)`: the row's own `refunded_cents` was neither in the computation nor even in the `for update` select, which read `id, charged_amount_cents, tax_amount_cents, settlement`. So the leftover was measured against the period's total capacity rather than its REMAINING capacity. For a refund that is harmless, because Stripe's `Charge.amount_refunded` is cumulative and the SQL takes `greatest(refunded_cents, inCharge)`. A CHARGEBACK is the opposite: `charge.dispute.created` states only the newly disputed part and the SQL ADDED it on top, capped at `least(charged_amount_cents, refunded_cents + coalesce(inCharge, charged_amount_cents))`. When `refunded_cents + inCharge` exceeds the charge, the cap silently swallowed the excess AND the leftover was 0, so `applyRefund`'s spill never ran and the `<invoice>:proration` row written by `prorationPeriodFor` stayed settled with `refunded_cents` 0. Since an invoice's provider amounts are scaled to charge + pending net + tax while the primary period's cap is its own charge alone, the sum routinely exceeds the cap on every deferred-proration invoice. IMPACT: on such an invoice a partial refund followed by a dispute of the un-refunded remainder claws back every cent the family paid while the owner's revenue page records only the subscription part — gross 4999, refunds 3999, net 1000 for money that is entirely gone. That is the hole the same docstring claimed to have closed, still open through the one ordering it did not name, and unreconcilable: Stripe shows 4999 reversed and PencilLift 3999, with no row the owner can point at. | Ruled out before filing: (1) the refund path is sound and separate — `Charge.amount_refunded` is cumulative and the partial/full branches use `greatest(...)`, so two successive partial refunds spill correctly (2000 then 4500 gives 501 then 801 on the proration row); the defect is confined to the ADD-on-top chargeback branch; (2) the primary really is capped and the excess really is lost, not deferred — `least(...)` with no second write, and `applyRefund` makes exactly one secondary call whose amount is `primary.leftoverCents`; (3) not rescued by a later event — the reversal branch only subtracts, and `pending_refunds` is never written for the derived id; (4) the repro does not depend on the unverified "Stripe disputes at most the un-refunded remainder" assumption, since webhooks.ts itself states Stripe allows partial disputes and any amount with `refunded_cents + inCharge > charged` reproduces it; (5) the $1 school donation is not the mechanism — `planAccruals` rejects the proration row through `evaluateDonationEligibility`'s subscription_period rule; (6) every other reader of `billing_periods` was checked (the revenue query, the 0690/0730/0740 school functions, p17-jobs, promotions-data, admin-ops) and none compensates. | Lead: CONFIRMED and fixed with C-2 as one change: the walk must spill on what the bucket has LEFT, not its size. FIX: `refunded_cents` and the new `chargeback_cents` joined the `for update` select. The walk now derives `refundedPartCents = max(0, refunded_cents - chargeback_cents)` and, for an event that states only the NEW part (`chargeback`/`chargeback_reversed`), gives the period only `max(0, charged_amount_cents - refundedPartCents)` plus that room's FLOORED share of the row's tax, handing the rest on as the leftover; the cumulative figures (`refund`, `partial_refund`) still measure against the whole bucket, because their figures are cumulative, and the docstring now says which provider figures are cumulative and which are incremental rather than letting one expression serve both silently. The cap is unchanged: every per-row figure is still at most that row's charge. | apps/api/tests/billing-r2.review.test.ts > 'C-PRORATION-REVERSAL: a provider refund reaches BOTH periods of one invoice' > '[HUNT7-C-1] a chargeback after a partial refund spills what the primary bucket has LEFT, not nothing', and the prose half '[HUNT7-C-1] the walk's docstring says which provider figures are cumulative and which are incremental'. MUTATION: none specific to this finding is recorded; `0d8fdf4` records that every fix in it was shown red first and mutation-checked with the production line reverted and each file restored with `sha256sum -c`. | — |
| BUG-364 | **High** / BILL-R1-1 / P17 donation reversal / L-053 — round 6's two money fixes each right alone and wrong together | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-C-2; fixed with HUNT7-C-1 and migration 0950 in `0d8fdf4` | A chargeback ADDED and capped — `least(charged_amount_cents, refunded_cents + coalesce(inCharge, charged_amount_cents))` — while a won dispute SUBTRACTED the whole converted amount, `greatest(0, refunded_cents - coalesce(inCharge, charged_amount_cents))`, which also decided the settlement. The round trip is exact only while `refunded_cents + inCharge <= charged_amount_cents`, and the round-6 unit conversion guarantees that only when the provider's charge total equals `charged + tax`, i.e. on an invoice with no other lines. HUNT6-C-1's `prorationPeriodFor` created exactly the invoice shape where it does not: the provider total is `charged + pending net + tax` while the primary's cap is `charged`. Tax 0 for clarity — charge 3999 with a 1000 ':proration' sibling; refund 1000 gives refunded 1000; dispute 3999 gives `least(3999, 1000+3999)` = 3999 with 1000 clipped; the win subtracts the unclipped 3999 and leaves 0 with settlement back to 'settled'. Because `refunded_cents` lands at exactly 0 with 'settled', `planAdjustment`'s `settledInFull` is true and the $1 school donation reversed at the refund is REINSTATED — which also falsifies the round-6 hunt's own not-a-false-positive note for HUNT6-C-2. Before this diff the same three events produced a correct aggregate, because the proration money was in no month's gross; adding it to gross without fixing the round trip is what made the figure wrong. IMPACT: a parent who received a partial refund and then lost a chargeback reads, on their own support case, "The store has not reported a refund on this charge yet" although the store refunded them — a false statement about their money on the one surface built to tell them what the provider reported, with the same 0 in the admin case detail. The owner's revenue page overstates net by the full proration amount, and a school is paid a $1 donation for a month in which the family was partly refunded, which is the one thing the P17 reversal exists to prevent. | Ruled out before filing: (1) it is a regression in its observable effect, not merely pre-existing — the primary row's arithmetic is unchanged, but before the diff gross 3999 / refunds 0 was the CORRECT aggregate and now gross is 4999 with refunds 0; (2) the new taxed round-trip case cannot see it, its fixture having no proration line (subtotal 4998, tax 412, amount_paid 5410), so the charge total equals `charged + tax` and the two halves convert to the cap exactly — and no test anywhere combined a dispute with a deferred-proration invoice; (3) HUNT7-C-1's fix does not close it, because with the spill corrected the win still subtracts the full converted 3999 from the primary's 3999 and erases the genuine refund — they are separate defects; (4) it holds under both readings of Stripe's dispute amount, since disputing the full 4999 still clips the primary and still subtracts 3999; (5) `planAdjustment` cannot double-reverse instead, a 'chargeback' returning null once 'reversal' is written, so the reinstatement is the only adjustment the win writes; (6) the row the parent sees is confirmed to be the primary one they would pick, support.ts joining on the case's channel and provider_period_id. | Lead: CONFIRMED, HIGH, and it is the two round-6 money fixes being each right alone and wrong together — a lesson in itself. TAKE THE SCHEMA FIX: migration number 0950, adding `chargeback_cents integer not null default 0` beside `refunded_cents`, so a chargeback stores what it added and its reversal subtracts exactly that. Fix it TOGETHER with C-1, which is the same walk. Do not weaken the cap or any BILL-R1-1 assertion. FIX: PREMISE: what a dispute added is not recoverable from a single column once the cap has clipped it, so it is STORED. Migration `0950_billing_period_chargeback.sql` adds `chargeback_cents` with `billing_periods_chargeback_cents_non_negative` and `billing_periods_chargeback_cents_within_refund`, so the invariant 0 <= chargeback_cents <= refunded_cents <= charged fails loudly rather than letting a reversal subtract money no dispute ever added; every pre-0950 row reads 0, which is the pre-0950 behaviour exactly. billing-sync.ts's update was rewritten around a `plan` CTE that computes `next_refund` and `next_chargeback` separately, writes `refunded_cents = least(plan.charged, plan.next_refund + plan.next_chargeback)` and `chargeback_cents = plan.next_chargeback`, and decides the settlement from `plan.next_chargeback` — so a chargeback adds `min(inCharge, charged - refundedPart)` and records it, and a won dispute gives back exactly that and no more, leaving an earlier genuine refund untouched. `planAdjustment`'s docstring now states what its rule does and does NOT establish: it reads the state handed in, and the caller owes it a settlement state that is true of the money. | apps/api/tests/billing-r2.review.test.ts > 'C-PRORATION-REVERSAL…' > '[HUNT7-C-2] winning that dispute gives back only what the dispute added, so the real refund survives and the $1 is not reinstated'. The column's own contract is pinned by supabase/tests/billing_tax.test.ts > '[HUNT7-C-2] public.billing_periods.chargeback_cents' (five cases: not-null integer defaulting to 0; 0 for a period written without it; stores the dispute's part; refuses a negative contribution; refuses one larger than the refund figure it is part of) and > '[HUNT7-C-2] chargeback_cents inherits billing_periods' RLS and grants' (four cases: a family member reads their own; another family reads no row; a child and anon cannot select the column at all; a parent cannot write it). MUTATION: none specific to this finding is recorded. | — |
| BUG-365 | Medium / L-037 (one new row, two surfaces) / spec P14 support; a parent must never guess which of two identical-looking charges is theirs | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-C-3; fixed in `0d8fdf4`, including the defect the area's checker found in the first fix | `prorationPeriodFor` writes the second row with a `...period` spread, so it inherits the subscription period's `period_start`, `period_end`, `paid_slots` and `settled_at` and differs only in `provider_period_id`, `kind` and `charged_amount_cents`. GET /v1/support/billing-periods selected every row for the family with no kind filter and ordered by `period_start desc, id desc`; both rows of one invoice share `period_start`, so the tie was broken by `id`, which is `gen_random_uuid()`, making the order between them random per family. `supportBillingPeriodSchema` was a `strictObject` with no `kind`, so neither client could label the row: the portal picker rendered `Stripe · Sep 19 – Oct 19 · $10.00` beside `Stripe · Sep 19 – Oct 19 · $39.99`, and the app rendered `Stripe · Sep 19 to Oct 19 · $10.00 for 1 child` — a sentence that is false about the row, that $10 being a mid-cycle item from the PREVIOUS period and not a period charge for those dates. The admin surface had been given the label and the admin contract the enum; the parent's had not. IMPACT: a parent opening a refund request for their $39.99 renewal sees two entries for the same dates and, half the time, the $10 one on top of a list they are told is newest-first. If they pick it, the case is linked to the proration period and every refund figure and settlement they then read is that row's, while staff assess a refund of the wrong amount against the wrong row. | Ruled out before filing: (1) the row is genuinely parent-visible — `billing_periods` has a member read policy, the query runs `asParent`, and 0810's refund-request check accepts it, only requiring the period to belong to the family; (2) a kind filter alone is not sufficient, because an immediately invoiced mid-cycle change is a real separate Stripe charge already recorded with kind 'proration', so filtering `kind = 'subscription_period'` would hide a charge a parent legitimately wants refunded; (3) the admin path is unaffected; (4) no existing test would catch it — apps/api/tests/support.test.ts seeded only subscription periods and the portal test supplied a single period; (5) the ordering tie is real rather than stable-by-insert, Postgres giving no order guarantee and both rows being inserted in the same transaction. | Lead: CONFIRMED. Add `kind` to the support billing-period contract (it is already in the admin one) and label it. A parent must never have to guess which of two identical-looking charges is theirs. FIX: packages/contracts/src/support.ts gained `billingPeriodKindSchema`, `SUPPORT_BILLING_PERIOD_KIND_LABELS`, `supportBillingPeriodKindNote` and `supportDerivedChargeLabel`, so ONE wording serves the portal and the app; `kind` joined `supportBillingPeriodSchema`; support.ts selects `p.kind as period_kind` on both the list and the case detail and orders by `period_start desc, split_part(provider_period_id, ':', 1) asc, …` so a derived row can never sort above its own charge. THE CHECKER GRADED THE PREMISE FALSE: the first fix applied a CORRECT label where it is false — a mid-cycle adjustment that arrives as its OWN invoice was told it was "billed with the <date> charge", asserting a billing relationship it does not have and throwing away the dates that are true of it. Derived-ness is now a fact the API STATES, `derivedFromProviderPeriodId`, and the contract's docstring forbids a surface inferring it from `kind` or from the shape of a provider id; both clients use the "billed with" label only where that field is non-null and otherwise keep the row's own dates with the kind as a qualifier. | apps/api/tests/support.test.ts > 'intake' > '[HUNT7-C-3] says what kind each period is, and never lists a derived proration row above its own charge'; apps/mobile/src/support/view-model.test.ts > '[HUNT7-C-3] names a derived mid-cycle row for what it is, not as a period charge for a child' and > '[HUNT7-C-3] a mid-cycle charge that is its OWN invoice keeps its own dates'; apps/web/src/pages/app/SupportPage.test.tsx > '[HUNT7-C-3] names the derived mid-cycle row in the refund picker instead of offering a second unnamed charge'. MUTATION: recorded in `seedPeriod`'s own comment in support.test.ts — with two random uuids the old `order by period_start desc, id desc` came out in the right order about three runs in five, so the case only failed on the mutation SOME of the time (the round-7 checker measured it); the derived row is now given an id that sorts ABOVE the subscription row's, which makes the old order fail every time. | — |
| BUG-366 | Low / N1-TAX-APPORTION / HUNT5-C-2 (one unit for every provider figure) / L-053 | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-C-4; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | `prorationPeriodFor` set `taxCents: 0` and justified it by the row's CHARGE already being pre-tax — but the figure that now reaches the row is the leftover, which the walk's own comment says is "still in the caller's unit", i.e. tax-inclusive. With `taxCents` 0, `preTaxRefundCents` short-circuits and returns it unchanged, so the one row whose whole purpose is to hold pre-tax collected money recorded refunds in the provider's unit. On the fixture the new cases use (subtotal 4999, tax 412, amount_paid 5411, giving primary 3999/tax 329 and proration 1000/tax 0 with an own share of 83), a partial refund of 4400 wrote 72 against the 1000 charge where the true pre-tax spill is 66: six cents too much. The direction was checked algebraically — the secondary is reached only above 4328, and `leftover − true spill` is non-negative for every amount above 4321 — so it always OVER-records the refund, i.e. understates net revenue, the safe direction, and it is bounded by the row's own cap so a full refund is exact. The same diff also falsified its own test comment, which told the next reader "a refund of this invoice is recorded against the subscription period, which is the row that holds the provider's figure" while three of its own cases assert `refunded_cents` 1000 on the proration row. IMPACT: the owner's refunds column is a few cents high on any partial refund large enough to spill onto a deferred-proration row, so a Stripe reconciliation is off by the proration item's tax share, and the parent's case detail shows the same few cents. The bigger cost is the prose: the justification was about the wrong side of the conversion, and a reader trusting the test comment would conclude no refund reaches that row at all. | Ruled out before filing: (1) the direction cannot be the unsafe one — proved above, so clawed-back money can never read as kept revenue through this path; (2) the figure cannot make net go negative, every row's `refunded_cents` being capped at its own charge; (3) a full refund is exact, the leftover being at least the pending net and the cap taking it back to the charge; (4) storing the row's real tax share does not double-count tax as revenue, `tax_amount_cents` being read only inside `applyRefund` and excluded from every other select; (5) no test pinned the six-cent behaviour, the only spill cases asserting a full refund or no spill, so correcting it breaks nothing. | Lead: CONFIRMED. Giving the ':proration' row `taxCents` 0 writes the tax-inclusive leftover straight into a field the rest of the system reads as tax-exclusive. Store the proration row's own tax or state in the row why it cannot be known; do not leave the unit ambiguous. FIX: the row carries its own FLOORED share of the invoice's tax — a `prorationTaxCents` helper apportions the invoice tax by the pending proration net, `min(tax, floor(tax * pendingNetCents / denominator))` — and `prorationPeriodFor` takes `taxCents: period.pendingProrationTaxCents`, so `preTaxRefundCents` restates the leftover on this row exactly as it does on the primary. The flooring is deliberate and stated: no row ever stores more tax than was levied on it, so the conversion can only over-record a refund, never under-record one. The row's own comment now justifies the figure by the unit of the AMOUNT that reaches it, not by the unit of its charge; the false test comment is gone; `0950` restates `tax_amount_cents`' column comment, whose 0900 wording named the subscription line alone; and ops-metrics.ts's owner-facing "every provider figure restated in that same pre-tax unit" now says why that promise holds on BOTH rows. | apps/api/tests/billing-r2.review.test.ts > '[HUNT7-C-4] both rows of one invoice carry their own floored share of its tax' and > '[HUNT7-C-4] a partial refund that spills is recorded on the proration row in that row's own unit'; the prose half is > '[HUNT7-C-4] prorationPeriodFor justifies its tax by the unit of the amount that reaches the row'. MUTATION: none specific to this finding is recorded. | — |
| BUG-367 | Low / CLAUDE.md "Records: read on resume, reconcile with Git/CI" / L-053 | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-C-5; the correction is made in this ledger-writing pass, not in `0d8fdf4` (which touched no doc) | BUG-305's row records the same fix twice — its own FIX column reports the first fixer's work, and two later Re-fix sections in the same row record C-PRORATION-REVERSAL and C-NEGATIVE-NET — but its residual column was never updated with them, so it still states as present-tense fact: "A refund/dispute names the invoice id, so it still reaches only the subscription period; I did not spread a refund onto the sibling row (see residuals) because that widens applyRefund and webhooks beyond the lead's decision." The shipped code does the opposite: `applyRefund` derives `<invoice>:proration` and applies the leftover to it, and apps/api/tests/billing-r2.review.test.ts asserts `refunded_cents` 1000 on that row in three cases. IMPACT: CLAUDE.md names this file as a record to read on resume and reconcile with Git, so that sentence is the first thing a round-8 finder or fixer reads about whether the proration row is reachable by a refund — and it sends them hunting a hole that is closed while the real holes were HUNT7-C-1 and HUNT7-C-2. It also hides that the feature was extended twice after its own fixer declined to extend it, which is exactly the history a reader needs in order to distrust it. | Ruled out before filing: (1) the code really does spread the refund, so the ledger and not the code is wrong — the suffix constant, the derived lookup and the three asserting cases were each read; (2) the residual is not merely historical framing, the sentence being present tense ("it still reaches only") in a row whose state is "closed (residual in Next)", i.e. presented as what remains true after the round; (3) no other record corrects it — `grep -rn 'C-PRORATION-REVERSAL' docs/` returns only that row; (4) the C-NEGATIVE-NET half IS recorded in the Re-fix text, so only the residual is stale — a one-sentence correction, not a rewrite. | Lead: CONFIRMED as prose: BUG-305's residual records the pre-fix behaviour as current. Lead writes the ledger; the fixer reports the exact sentence. FIX: BUG-305's residual column is replaced with what is now true — the refund walk exists (primary first, in its own unit and capped, leftover to the `<invoice>:proration` row, nothing parked against the derived id) — and it names the two residuals that really remained when it was written, HUNT7-C-1 and HUNT7-C-2, now closed as BUG-363 and BUG-364 above. The stale sentence is not retained anywhere. | No test covers the ledger, and the finding says so. The check is textual and is the finding's own: BUG-305's residual asserted a property of apps/api/src/services/billing-sync.ts that apps/api/tests/billing-r2.review.test.ts > 'a fully refunded renewal reverses its deferred proration too, so net is zero' disproves, its `expect(rows[1]).toMatchObject({ provider_period_id: `<invoice>:proration`, settlement: 'refunded', refunded_cents: 1000 })` passing today. | — |
| BUG-368 | Medium / APL-07 / PLAY-10 account closure copy / L-050 (say what an operation really reports) — the same defect as round 6's inert close-flow fix, one level in | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-K-1; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | `accountClosedDeviceMessage` takes the clean `ACCOUNT_CLOSE_COPY.closed`/`.pending` branch when `device.secretsCleared` is true and otherwise adds "This device is signed out, but we could not remove everything this app had saved for your account. Removing the app removes the rest." `secretsCleared` came from `clearDeviceAdultSecrets`, which round 6 converted from `.catch(() => undefined)` to `.then(() => true, () => false)` on two calls — but only the OUTER wrappers, and both still swallowed the failure of the operation the flag NAMES. `clearBiometricPin` opened with `await SecureStore.deleteItemAsync(BIOMETRIC_PIN_KEY).catch(() => undefined)`, so a refused delete of the PIN ITEM was discarded and the function still resolved as long as the two plain flag deletes succeeded — `pinCleared` therefore proved "the two flags were deleted", the fact ADJACENT to "the PIN was removed". `forgetStoreIdentity` ended in `await Purchases.logOut().catch(() => undefined)`, so `storeForgotten` proved "a module-local map was cleared and the mutex resolved". The composite was true whenever the two SecureStore flag deletes resolved, which is the same "no keychain write threw" predicate HUNT6-J-1 was filed to remove. IMPACT: on a device whose keychain refuses the PIN item specifically, or whose store SDK logout fails, a parent who has just deleted their account reads "Your PencilLift account is closed and this device is signed out." and is NOT shown the caveat the round added for exactly that case, so they leave the app installed with their 6-digit parent PIN still in the device keychain and the store SDK still bound to the closed account's app user id. | Ruled out before filing: (1) the leftover PIN is not directly usable — `clearBiometricPin` deletes BIOMETRIC_ENABLED_KEY before returning, so the biometric offer is withheld — which bounds the finding to a retained secret plus a withheld caveat but does not remove it, the caveat existing precisely to tell the parent something stayed behind; (2) a keychain-wide fault WOULD report correctly, the flags going through the same `deleteItemAsync`, so the hole is the asymmetric case and it is the plausible one, BIOMETRIC_PIN_KEY being the only item written with `requireAuthentication: true` and WHEN_PASSCODE_SET_THIS_DEVICE_ONLY and expo-secure-store documenting deletes as able to reject; (3) the store half self-heals only for the store, `identifiedAs` being nulled before the logOut, and the parent is still not told; (4) the sibling flag `sessionEndConfirmed` really does observe the session removal (auth-js 2.116 `_signOut` → `removeCurrentSession` → an unguarded `removeItemAsync`, and chunked-storage's `removeItem` propagates every rejection), so this is not a blanket complaint; (5) no test covered it — screens-r2.review.test.ts only grepped that `clearDeviceAdultSecrets` mentions `biometricPinStore.clear()`, and parent-privacy.test.ts fed `secretsCleared: false` in directly rather than producing it. | Lead: CONFIRMED and it is the SAME defect as round 6's inert close-flow fix, one level in: `secretsCleared` reports that neither secret was removed because both clears still swallow the failure that matters. Make the helpers answer; keep best-effort at the caller. FIX: PREMISE: each half of the flag must be asked of the operation the flag NAMES, not of its wrapper. `clearBiometricPin` returns whether the PIN ITEM's own delete succeeded and still never throws and still never stops the two flag deletes; `forgetStoreIdentity` returns a boolean, remembering a refused `Purchases.logOut()` in a module-level `storeIdentityMayRemain` until a later bind replaces the identity — needed because `identifiedAs` is nulled before the logOut is awaited, so without it the SECOND `forgetStoreIdentity` of a sign-out (the one the closure screen reads) would answer "nothing to forget" for a device whose SDK is still bound to the closed account. `biometricPinStore.clear()` keeps a best-effort face for the callers that only want the enrolment gone (the store's own 'other_user' cleanup, unlock.ts's stale-PIN removal, the unlock screen's Face ID toggle), and `clearDeviceAdultSecrets` calls the helper itself, keeping the outer `.then(…, () => false)` as a belt for a rejection neither is supposed to produce. mode.ts's `DeviceSignOutOutcome` docstring now says what each half observed. | apps/mobile/src/family/screens-r2.review.test.ts > 'a closed account's device secrets are reported, not assumed (HUNT7-K-1)', three cases: '[repro] a keychain that refuses the PIN item answers secretsCleared: false', '[repro] a store SDK that refuses to forget the identity answers false too', and 'a device that gave up both secrets says so, and the copy is the clean one'. The store half's own contract is pinned by apps/mobile/src/billing/store.test.ts > '[repro] a refused logOut is reported, and stays reported until a later bind succeeds' and > 'a device that was never bound has no identity to fail to forget'. | BUG-369 | Medium / child-facing copy must be true while it is shown; HUNT6-J-2's principle applied to the child surface | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-K-2; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | In the scan screen's `send()` abort branch nothing changed state between the child's tap and the awaited cancel: the Stop button was `onPress={() => controllerRef.current?.abort()}`, `abort()` sets no React state, and the outcome was set only after `await cancelScan(childApi, attemptRef.current)` answered. So for the whole cancel round trip the screen still rendered `upload.kind === 'running'` — the frozen progress line "Sending page 2 of 3…", the progress bar, and the same live "Stop sending" button. That POST goes through `childApi` with no request options, so it runs to `DEFAULT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` (20,000 ms) before answering 'unsure', and `childApi` is `withChildTokenRetry(...)`, which re-runs the call once on a single UNAUTHENTICATED answer, so a second full attempt can follow. Because `running` is also what hides the page controls and the Send button, the child could do nothing else either, and the only control on screen was the one that now does nothing, a second `abort()` on an aborted controller being a no-op. IMPACT: a child aged 5–13 taps "Stop sending" on a flaky connection and the screen goes on telling them their homework is being sent, with Stop still offered, for up to twenty seconds per attempt — the "nothing happened, press it again" experience HUNT6-J-2 identified on the parent closure screen — and the progress text is false while it is shown, the send having already been aborted locally. | Ruled out before filing: (1) no money harm — the Send/"Try again" button is not rendered while `running`, so no second `send()` and no second assignment can start from this window, and repeated `abort()` calls cannot issue a second cancel, which is why it was filed medium and not high; (2) the window is real rather than instantaneous, being skipped only when `attempt.assignmentId === null`, where `cancelScan` returns 'nothing_to_cancel' with no request — so the delay happens exactly when a scan really was created; (3) the 20 s figure is not overridden, `createMobileApi` passing no options and the cancel passing no per-request timeout; (4) no other state acknowledges the tap — there is no 'stopping' state in `UploadState` and `setNotice` is not called on abort; (5) not out of scope as pre-existing, the `await cancelScan` being what round 5 put there and round 6 re-read and re-documented, and the round-6 diff having fixed the identical shape on the parent surface in the same commit. | Lead: CONFIRMED. FIX: PREMISE: the ORDER has to be runnable in a test rather than grepped, which is why the decision moved out of the screen — the same move `runAccountClosure` made for the parent screen (the previous check grepped the screen for the await and the ternary, which both orderings satisfy). The abort path became a function in apps/mobile/src/homework/upload.ts that emits a `stopping` step BEFORE the cancel is awaited and then hands the caller the outcome `cancelScan` earns; the screen gained the matching state, rendering calm child copy with no progress claim and no live Stop button, and offering no second send, no new page and no reordering while a scan is stopping. The screen half is apps/mobile/src/homework/scan-screen.test.ts > 'the child is answered the moment they stop a scan (HUNT7-K-2)', three cases: '[repro] the tap has a state of its own, set before the cancel answers', 'says only that it is stopping: no progress claim, and no button that can do nothing', and 'offers no second send, no new page and no reordering while a scan is stopping'. | BUG-370 | Low / L-053 when code and prose disagree, decide which is TRUE | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-K-3; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | parent-auth.ts's `signOut` JSDoc said "when the logout call failed it does not remove the local session either — so dropping that `error` left a device still holding the parent's session with nothing in the app able to tell. `ok: false` means this device may still be signed in." The middle clause is false for the case it describes. In auth-js 2.116 `_signOut`, when `admin.signOut(accessToken, scope)` returns an error that is not 404/401/403/session-missing and the scope is not 'others' (this app passes 'local'), it calls `removeCurrentSession()` and only THEN returns `{ error }`; a fetch failure takes the same route, `AuthRetryableFetchError` not being an `AuthApiError`. So the session is gone from the keychain in both failure shapes the file's own tests exercise. The only path that returns an error WITHOUT removing is the early `sessionError` return — the stored session could not be read or refreshed — which is a different fact from "the logout call failed". Three other places in the tree state it correctly and contradict this file: parent-auth.test.ts's own comment and its assertion that `parentAuth.userId()` is null after a 500 on /logout, mode.ts's `DeviceSignOutOutcome`, and parent-privacy.ts. IMPACT: no runtime effect. The harm is to the next reader of the only wrapper that decides what the app may claim about a signed-out device: the sentence justified `ok: false` as "this device may still be signed in", which is the overstatement HUNT6-J-1's own fix removed from the parent-facing copy, so a maintainer reasoning from this file rather than from mode.ts would put it back. | Ruled out before filing: (1) the installed library was read rather than recalled, in both the client and the admin API, tracing the HTTP-error and the fetch-rejection paths; (2) the sentence is not salvaged by an edge case — the early `sessionError` return does leave the stored session in place, so the FINAL sentence is defensible, which is why the middle clause was filed and the fix is a rewording; (3) the code itself is right, `signOut` returning `{ok:false}` for both a returned `{error}` and a thrown error and `signOutParent` consuming `.ok` without re-swallowing it, so the reported fact reaches the screen; (4) no test pins the wrong sentence, parent-auth.test.ts asserting on behaviour, so a reword turns nothing red. | Lead: CONFIRMED as prose: the JSDoc states the opposite of what auth-js does and of what its own test says. FIX: the JSDoc now says supabase-js reports a refused or failed sign-out in `{ error }` rather than throwing, and then, under "What it does with the stored session, since this is the file a maintainer reads for it", that with scope 'local' auth-js 2.116 STILL removes the local session when the logout call failed — `_signOut` calls `removeCurrentSession()` and only then returns the error, for an HTTP failure and a fetch failure alike — and that the one path that returns an error without removing is the early `sessionError` return, a different fact. It concludes that `ok: false` means this device cannot say the session ended, either because the service was never told or because the session could not be read at all, and is deliberately NOT "this device is still signed in", which is the adjacent claim the copy may not make. | apps/mobile/src/family/screens-r2.review.test.ts > '[repro] the sign-out doc says what auth-js does with the local session, and claims no more'. The behavioural counterpart is the pre-existing apps/mobile/src/lib/parent-auth.test.ts case that makes /logout answer 500, gets `ok:false` and then asserts `parentAuth.userId()` is null — the file's own suite having contradicted the file's own comment. MUTATION: none specific to this finding is recorded. | — |

ACCOUNT_CLOSE_COPY.closed : ACCOUNT_CLOSE_COPY.pending`, and `ACCOUNT_CLOSE_COPY.closed` is "Your PencilLift account is closed and this device is signed out." Its one production caller is `runAccountClosure`, so for the two success statuses that `message` was unreachable output — the pre-HUNT5-N6 sentence still sitting in the function a future caller would naturally read, still blessed by a JSDoc line presenting the status alone as meaning the device signs out. The comment in place of the old assertion records why it was pinning shape and not property. MUTATION: none specific to this finding is recorded. | — |
| BUG-372 | Medium / spec P4 deletion stops processing / FL-R4-04 (a 404 that claims a profile the parent can still see does not exist) / L-037 one rule two surfaces | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-J-2; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | `ownedChild` excludes any child with a `requested`/`processing` deletion request from BOTH access modes and throws NOT_FOUND before it looks at the archived flag, so GET /v1/children/:id/subjects and GET /v1/children/:id/learning-schedule both answer 404 for a deletion-pending child — while GET /v1/family deliberately keeps listing that child, so the mobile planner put it in `children` and selected it by default when it was first, the only choice in a one-child family. The child picker labels the state through a shared `childPickerSuffix` in src/family/family-view.ts (' (data deletion under way)' tested FIRST, before ' (archived — plan is read-only)', because `request_deletion` archives a child-scope target), so the parent is not left to discover it after choosing. | BUG-374 | Medium / HUNT6-I-2 and BUG-335 (an answer has an owner) / RV-rewards-7 (no button stays on screen that can only fail) / WEB-R2-01's phone equivalent | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-J-4; fixed in `0d8fdf4` | The parent Rewards screen owns its fetch instead of using the shared `useLoad`, and round 6 gave it only half of HUNT6-I-2: `setState({ status: 'loading' })` before `void load()` in the mount/identity effect, while `load` itself was still `setState({ status: 'ready', data: await loadRewardsOverview(api) })` with no run counter — the unguarded publish the same commit removed from `useLoad` and from the privacy screen. Four callers can overlap: the effect, pull-to-refresh, the error retry and the post-decision reload; and the post-decision path needs no unlucky timing, because `decide` clears `busy` BEFORE awaiting its reload, so every button is live again while that reload is in flight and approving a second card starts a second decision and a second `load()`. Whichever request resolved last won, so reload #1 could overwrite reload #2 and restore the list as it was before the second decision, with Approve/Decline still live on a request the server had already decided — the state the reload exists to prevent. The same unguarded publish is the privacy leg the round-6 prose claimed to have closed: when the gate publishes a new client for a new adult the effect starts B's load, and A's still-in-flight answer — fetched with A's bearer — publishes A's child nicknames, point balances and open reward requests as 'ready' under B's client. IMPACT: a parent approving two reward requests in quick succession can be shown the pre-decision list with buttons that can only fail, and on a handed-on tablet the previous family's child nicknames and point balances render for the next adult. | Ruled out before filing: (1) the screen really does key on the gate's client, so the identity half is not also missing — `RewardApprovals({ api })` receives `access.api` and `load` is a `useCallback` on `[api]`, which the keying walker does check; (2) RefreshControl does serialise the pulls, which is why the mechanism rests on the decide→reload path, where nothing serialises; (3) `decideRequestAction` does not re-render from its own response, returning only `{ok, needsPin, reload, message}`, so the list comes solely from `load`; (4) the old client's request would not simply fail after a handover, `createMobileApi` resolving the bearer when the request is made, so a request already in flight carries the previous adult's token and the server answers that family; (5) the privacy screen and `useLoad` both received the ticket, confirming this was an omission on one screen rather than a deliberate scope. | Lead: CONFIRMED and it is half a fix shipped: rows dropped before the fetch but no run ticket, so an older in-flight answer publishes over a newer one. Give it the ticket its sibling screens got. FIX: the screen holds a `latestLoad` ref; `load` bumps it and captures the ticket BEFORE anything is awaited, and returns without publishing when `latestLoad.current !== ticket` — for the rows and for the error alike. The comment records both doors: the decide→reload overlap (`busy` cleared before the reload is awaited) and the handed-on-tablet publish under the new adult's client. | apps/mobile/src/rewards/review.test.ts > 'the parent rewards screen publishes only its newest load (HUNT7-J-4)': '[repro] the ticket is taken before the await and checked before every publish' and 'keeps the other half: the rows go before the fetch, not when it answers (HUNT6-I-2)', which pins the round-6 half the round-7 fix must not undo. | BUG-375 | Low / L-054 (a guard must be able to see the shape it exists to catch) / BUG-335 and HUNT6-I-1 | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-J-5; fixed in `0d8fdf4`, including the defect the area's checker found in the first fix | The keying guard's own sentence was "the premise is asserted rather than assumed, over EVERY parent screen … A screen added later cannot opt out silently", while its walker scanned only `/\buse(?:Callback\|Effect)\(/` in app/(parent)/*.tsx and looked only for the bare identifiers `api` and `load`. Three ways to satisfy it while breaking the property: a screen-local loader hook whose body lives OUTSIDE app/(parent)/, since the walker only reads that directory — which is the shape HUNT6-I-1 found in the planner, differing only in where the helper is declared; a `useMemo` loader, not in the regex, and the privacy screen already builds its client in a `useMemo`; and a client bound under another name (`client`, `parentApi`, a destructured `{ api: gateApi }`). The companion guard meant to stop a vacuous pass named only four of the thirteen files in app/(parent)/, and the three that genuinely do not use `useLoad` were not in it — so the set it vouched for was the set that already passed. IMPACT: the enforcement BUG-335 was closed with was narrower than its own sentence, so a future screen, or a refactor that moves a loader helper into src/, could reinstate the handed-on-tablet defect with a green suite. No parent is harmed today: all thirteen files were enumerated and every data loader currently keys on the gate's client, or on the module token source for the privacy screen. | Ruled out before filing: (1) the test is not merely decorative — reverting the planner to its round-5 `useApiLoad` does make it red, so it catches the literal regression it was written for, the defect being the gap between that and the sentence it states; (2) no current screen already exploits a gap, which would have made it medium — `useMemo` is used for a store, a viewport, a locale and a view model, never for a fetch; (3) the source-mangling helper could not make the walker miss a real hook, stripping comments and quoted strings but not template literals, and the templates in these screens are balanced; (4) the walker does cover the three non-`useLoad` screens for the hooks they DO use, so the name-based check is load-bearing for them and the finding is about the shapes it cannot see. | Lead: CONFIRMED. The keying guard says no parent screen can opt out silently while inspecting only two hook shapes and one identifier. A guard that a useMemo loader walks past is not a guard — and a useMemo loader is exactly what HUNT6-I-1 missed. FIX: the walker became `loadHookOffenders(source)`, which considers ANY hook whose body fetches (`FETCHES = /\.(?:get\|send)\(\|…load[A-Z]\w*\|load…/`) or whose name is a loader hook (`LOADER_HOOK = /^use\w*(?:Load\|Query\|Fetch\|Api)\w*$/`), and DERIVES the client identifiers from the screen — whatever `access.api` is bound to, plus every hook binding whose own body fetches — instead of hard-coding 'api'/'load'. The companion guard now requires the shared hook in every screen that makes a request and names the deliberate exceptions with their own per-screen assertion. THE CHECKER GRADED THE PREMISE FALSE and found the first version WEAKER than the round-6 walker it replaced: it excused any hook that used a name something else recomputed, whatever that hook's own dependency array said, so `useEffect(() => void load(), [])` passed where the round-6 walker flagged it — blind to BUG-335's own shape. The excuse now holds only for a hook with NO dependency array of its own (a custom hook call such as `useLoad(loadFamily)`, which keys on its argument internally). | apps/mobile/src/family/screens-r2.review.test.ts: '[repro] no parent screen has a load hook that a new client cannot re-run' over the real screens, '[repro] the walker sees the shapes this defect can come back in' over SYNTHETIC screens (not files in the tree) with a correctly-keyed positive control first so "not equal []" cannot pass for the wrong reason, and 'the screens really do go through the shared hook, or are named exceptions with their own guard'. MUTATION: recorded in `0d8fdf4`'s message — the lead's own first cut of the walker's self-tests had the same weakness, the two shapes added being flagged for an unrelated reason and passing under BOTH walkers, so they were not evidence for the fix at all; binding the client to a name, as the real screens do, made them discriminate, and the mutation now fails naming the shape. | — |

FIX: the bump and the capture are now the FIRST thing `load` does, before the `closureStarted` and `!api` returns — the same ordering `useLoad` uses, where the ticket is taken before anything can be awaited — so EVERY entry into `load` supersedes what is in flight, including the two that publish nothing. | BUG-378 | **High** / WEB-R2-01 / BUG-322 and BUG-323 (the portal may not say "signed out" over a live session) / L-058 — the third consecutive round with a high finding in this area | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-F-1; the F7-5-web-auth area was deliberately held out of `0d8fdf4` because its checker had not finished, and shipped in `b972ef8` | Round 6's remedy was that the adapter stops claiming this origin's session is gone and every caller that would say so re-reads `currentSession()`. That read cannot establish the fact, and on the one refusal path that matters it returned the wrong answer. (1) When localStorage is unusable auth-js does not keep the session in localStorage at all: with `persistSession: true` and no `storage` option it uses `globalThis.localStorage` only when `supportsLocalStorage()` is true, and otherwise its `memoryLocalStorageAdapter` — and that is false in TWO ways, touching the property throwing, and the object being readable while a test write throws ("block site data" in Chrome and Safari). `forgetStoredSession` only ever called `globalThis.localStorage?.removeItem(storageKey)`, so in the first variant it threw into the catch and in the second it succeeded as a no-op on a key nobody had written; either way the session auth-js was actually using was untouched. (2) The refusal path that leaves a usable refresh token is the pre-flight-refresh path, where `_signOut` returns `sessionError` before `removeCurrentSession()` and `_callRefreshToken` skips `_removeSession()` for a retryable fetch error — so the session was still in the memory store with a valid refresh token. (3) `currentSession()` destructured only `data` and dropped the `error`; the refusal was caused by a refresh failure, `_callRefreshToken` caches that failure for 60 s, so the very next `getSession()` resolved `{ data: { session: null }, error }` and `currentSession()` mapped it to `null`. `stillSignedIn()`'s catch, whose comment says "The session cannot be read, so it cannot be shown as ended either", never ran, because auth-js resolves rather than throwing — the round-6 defect verbatim, a recovery inside a `catch` the real adapter never enters, moved one level down into the read. (4) So `open === false` and the portal navigated to /sign-in carrying `SIGN_OUT_NOT_TOLD_COPY.signInOpen`, "This computer is signed out." (5) The session then came back in that same tab: the 30 s auto-refresh ticker refreshes from the memory store as soon as the fetch succeeds, writes the rotated session back and emits TOKEN_REFRESHED, which flips `useParentSession` back to `signed_in` — and /sign-in's brand bar carries a "Parent portal" link. IMPACT: a parent on a shared family, school or library computer — exactly the locked-down browser BUG-322's own recorded impact names — presses Sign out while offline or during an auth outage, reads "This computer is signed out", and leaves; when the network returns, one click gives the next person the family's children, homework scans, verdicts, points, safety reports, devices and guardian emails, and the parent area is still unlocked because the `POST /v1/adult/lock` step-up failed for the same offline reason. | Ruled out before filing: (a) the ordinary localStorage case is fine, the removal really removing the key, so the claim is specific to auth-js's memory storage; (b) `storageKey` cannot be null while the adapter lives, the adapter's formula matching supabase-js's own and a URL it cannot parse also making `validateSupabaseUrl` throw inside `createClient`; (c) auth-js DOES clear its memory store on the other refusal path, so the hole needs the expired-token pre-flight path, and that path leaves storage alone; (d) nothing else clears it — `signOutOnUnauthenticated` needs an HTTP answer and so does not fire while offline, and nothing else calls signOut; (e) the existing tests could not cover it, both blocked-storage cases running on a fake whose `getSession` decides for itself and the real-library block only ever running with a working localStorage; (f) `window` 'storage' events would not help, auth-js registering only 'visibilitychange'. | Lead: CONFIRMED, HIGH, and this area is now on its THIRD consecutive round with a high finding — which by L-058 is the feature telling us its premise is wrong. Do NOT patch it a third time. TAKE THE DESIGN CHANGE. FIX: PREMISE: the adapter cannot report on a store it does not own, and "answering `session: null`" is not the same fact as "the session is gone". (1) `SessionStore` was added and `createSessionStore()` hands `portalClient` a store the adapter holds: this origin's localStorage when a write probe proves it readable AND writable — the same two tests auth-js applies, so the store chosen is the store auth-js would have chosen, the difference being only that this adapter holds it — otherwise the adapter's own Map. Reads and writes are deliberately NOT swallowed, so a read that throws reaches `getSession()` and comes back as unreadable. `forgetStoredSession(store, storageKey)` now ANSWERS `'removed' \| 'absent' \| 'kept'`. The step-up client is handed no store, `persistSession: false` making auth-js ignore the option, because a second writer on the portal's slot is what WEB-R2-04 exists to prevent. (2) `readSession()` keeps auth-js's `error` and returns a `SessionRead` of `signed_in \| signed_out \| unreadable`; a session present but carrying no access token or no email is `unreadable` too, since it exists so this browser is not signed out, and it cannot be used so it is not a session. (3) A single `stillSignedIn(auth)` in lib/auth.ts, shared by SignOutControl and PrivacyControlsPage so the two surfaces cannot drift, treats `unreadable` as STILL SIGNED IN — which is what both surfaces' own comments already said they intended — and falls back to `currentSession()` for the small test adapters. | apps/web/src/App.signout.test.tsx: '[HUNT7-F-1] stays on the portal page when the session could not be read, not just when it answered'; '[HUNT7-F-1] answers "unreadable", not "signed out", when the session could not be read' and '[HUNT7-F-1] answers "signed out" for a read that came back empty and clean'; and the describe '[HUNT7-F-1] a refused sign-out in a browser that cannot use localStorage', whose cases are 'leaves no session for the network to bring back', 'hands auth-js a store this adapter can clear when localStorage cannot be touched' and 'keeps using this origin's localStorage when it works'. | BUG-379 | Medium / WEB-R4-AUTH-2 (a report about the server must be derived from the server) / spec P14 truthful account copy | closed (residual in Next) | round-7 hunt HUNT7-F-2; held out of `0d8fdf4` with its area and shipped in `b972ef8` | The adapter's signOut was `const { error } = await auth.signOut({ scope }); if (!error) return; forgetStoredSession(storageKey); return { serverNotTold: true };` — every error auth-js resolves with became "the server was never told". One class of those errors proves the opposite. `_signOut` returns `sessionError` from `__loadSession`, which produces an error whenever a refresh of an expired session fails, INCLUDING a non-retryable failure, i.e. the auth server rejecting the refresh token; auth-js maps only `session_not_found` to `AuthSessionMissingError`, the one error `_signOut` ignores, while GoTrue answers a revoked or already-used refresh token with `refresh_token_not_found`/`invalid_grant`, which becomes a plain `AuthApiError`. So: access token expired, refresh token revoked server-side (the parent changed their password elsewhere, an admin revoked the session, or the `pending` account closure's family purge has just closed their sign-in) → the 400 makes `_callRefreshToken` call `_removeSession()` → `__loadSession` returns `{ session: null, error }` → `_signOut` returns it → the adapter reported `serverNotTold: true`. Nothing distinguished "we could not reach the server" from "the server refused our credential because it had already ended this session". IMPACT: a parent whose session the auth service had already destroyed presses Sign out and is told, in a role="alert" above the sign-in form, "We could not tell PencilLift's servers to end the session, so change your password if you are worried" — asked to take a security action over a session that provably no longer exists, and told their sign-out half-failed when it fully succeeded. On the account-closure route the same report travels to /account-deletion, so on the `pending` path a parent reads it on the screen confirming their deletion, moments after the purge closed the very sign-in whose password they are being told to change. | Ruled out before filing: (a) auth-js does not fold this into its ignorable set — `_signOut` skips only 404/401/403 from `admin.signOut` and `AuthSessionMissingError`, which is raised only for the `session_not_found` code that /logout returns, not what /token returns for a bad refresh token, so the refresh-rejection error really does reach the adapter as a plain error; (b) the window is not unreachable — the auto-refresh ticker does eventually emit SIGNED_OUT, but auth-js's proactive-preserve branch keeps the session alive with no event for the whole margin while the refresh is already failing, and the 60 s failure cache returns the cached failure without removing anything, so the control is still on screen and pressable; (c) the copy really is shown on this path, the early return hiding the control but not the navigation, and SignInPage rendering the notice from router state regardless of session state; (d) the over-warning is in the cautious direction, no data being exposed, which is why it was filed medium. | Lead: CONFIRMED and fixed with F-1: report the refusal from the fact that the server was not reached, not from 'auth-js returned something'. An error proving the service had ALREADY ended the session is the opposite of serverNotTold. FIX: `SESSION_ALREADY_ENDED_CODES` (`session_not_found`, `session_expired`, `refresh_token_not_found`) and `serviceEndedTheSession(error)`, which reads two facts auth-js itself constructs — `name`, being how auth-js tells its own error classes apart, and the code — so an `AuthApiError` is only ever built from an answer the SERVICE gave, a request that got no answer being an `AuthRetryableFetchError` instead. The report is decided from the error alone and BEFORE the removal and the teardown: `const report: SignOutReport = serviceEndedTheSession(error) ? undefined : { serverNotTold: true }`. Anything else — offline, a 5xx, a rate limit, a refusal that does not say the session ended — keeps the warning. lib/auth.ts's `SignOutRefused` docstring is corrected to say the report is derived from the service not being reached and not from an error coming back, and the outcome is reworded from "a sign-out the auth service did not carry out" to "was not told to carry out". | apps/web/src/App.signout.test.tsx > the WEB-R4-AUTH-2 adapter block: '[HUNT7-F-2] reports nothing to the parent when the auth service had already ended the session', '[HUNT7-F-2] still warns when the auth service could not be reached at all' and '[HUNT7-F-2] warns when the service answered but did not end the session'. MUTATION: none specific to this finding is recorded. | `refresh_token_already_used` is deliberately left OUT of `SESSION_ALREADY_ENDED_CODES` and so keeps the warning: it can mean the same thing, but it is also what a token another tab has just rotated answers, and whether the service revoked the rest of the session with it is not something this file can establish. The adapter's own comment records that choice as the cautious direction. |

| BUG-382 | Low / L-053; L-054 (an enumeration a fixer will act on must be complete) | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-F-5; held out of `0d8fdf4` with its area and shipped in `b972ef8` | `forgetStoredSession`'s docstring said a refusal leaves a stored session on "exactly one path: the stored access token has ALREADY EXPIRED", and excused the other case with "(auth-js also drops the fallback when storage changed under the refresh, but then the stored session is already gone and this has nothing to remove.)" `__loadSession`'s proactive-preserve branch hands the stored session back with `error: null` only while the access token is still valid AND the slot still holds the SAME refresh token, and that guard fails in two ways, not one: storage was CLEARED, and storage was REPLACED — another tab's refresh rotating the slot through `_saveSession`. IMPACT: no user is harmed by the removal itself, the parent having pressed Sign out — but the enumeration is what a fixer reads to decide which paths need a removal at all, and it told them one path exists where two do. | BUG-384 | Low / L-053; L-054 (a regression test must measure the property, not a proxy) / HUNT6-G-1 | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-I-5; held out of `0d8fdf4` with its area and shipped in `b972ef8` | The replace-instead-of-push comment justified itself with "this page needs a signed-in parent and re-reads the session to render (RequireParent), and on the `pending` path that session is deliberately still valid until the family purge finishes — so one Back used to put the next person at a shared computer back inside the parent portal". The navigation is reached only when the LOCAL session is gone: either the adapter reported nothing, which it does only after auth-js removed the session, or it reported a refusal and `stillSignedIn()` answered false. `RequireParent` does not read the server-side sign-in; it reads THIS browser's session through `useParentSession`, whose state starts 'loading' and is refreshed by an effect on every mount, and a Back navigation remounts the route element, so the read runs again, returns null, and the page renders SignInPrompt. The comment's mechanism therefore required a surviving local session, which this branch had just excluded — it conflated the server-side sign-in that `pending` keeps alive with the stored session RequireParent actually reads, the exact conflation HUNT6-G-1 corrected thirty lines above. Separately, `replace: true` removes only the /app/privacy entry, while a real browser history also holds /app and /app/children, so if the premise were true one more Back would re-enter the portal anyway. The test could not see this because its memory router is built with `initialEntries: ['/app/privacy']`, so after a replace the history has a single entry and `router.navigate(-1)` is clamped to it — the assertion measured history depth, not whether the portal renders. IMPACT: no parent or child is harmed, the property holding for a different reason and replace being right regardless. The cost is to the next reader and to the records: a comment stating a shared-computer hole the code refutes, and a regression test that would keep passing on an app whose history holds any other portal entry. | Ruled out before filing: (1) no navigating path leaves the session alive — the success path does not re-read, but the adapter returns void only when auth-js's signOut resolved with no error, which removes the stored session, and the unconfigured adapter cannot reach this code because RequireParent renders the 'not available' notice instead; (2) RequireParent really re-reads rather than trusting a cached shell state, `useParentSession` holding its own state per consumer and each page wrapping itself in its own RequireParent, so a remount always re-reads, the stale-shell problem affecting only the header line, which is not a gate; (3) the test is not vacuous about what it does test — with push it would land on /app/privacy and fail, so it does pin replace and simply does not pin reachability; (4) the change should not be reverted, replace being the right call. | Lead: CONFIRMED. The justification rests on a Back that cannot happen and the test measures history depth in a one-entry router. Either justify it from something real or drop it; and make the test measure the portal's router. FIX: the comment is cut to "Replaced, not pushed: the closed account's own page is not left one Back away (HUNT6-G-1)", and then states what actually keeps the portal shut — this branch is reached only when this browser's session is gone, and every portal page renders behind its own `RequireParent`, which re-reads THIS browser's session on each mount, so a Back remounts and the sign-in prompt is what renders. It records the withdrawn reason as the conflation of the server-side sign-in `pending` keeps alive with the stored session RequireParent reads, and adds that a real browser history holds /app and the other portal entries too, so replacing one entry could never have been the protection. | apps/web/src/pages/app/PrivacyControlsPage.test.tsx > '[HUNT6-G-1 / HUNT7-I-5] a Back after the closure reaches no portal content', which is the reworked case: the memory router is given more than the one entry and a route behind RequireParent, and after the Back the assertion is that no portal content renders and the sign-in prompt does — the property — rather than that the history has no entry to go back into. MUTATION: none specific to this finding is recorded. | — |
| BUG-385 | High / COPPA §312.5(b)(2) verification method, spec P3 | closed (the vendor half is an owner action) | owner directive: "add the license and face checker for parent … use ChatGPT to check the license against the picture confirm it's the same person and then delete" | PencilLift confirmed no ADULT. Migration 0970 records a per-child parent/guardian ATTESTATION, which is a claim by whoever is holding the account; nothing established that they are an adult, or that they are the person on any document. The owner's flow — photograph a government ID, take a selfie, check the document and the face, DELETE the images, then children, then payment, then one code per child — had no implementation at all. IMPACT: an under-age account holder, or an adult using someone else's identity, reached the same product as a verified parent, and the company had no evidence of an adult behind any family. | Ruled out before filing: (a) the store receipt carries no age signal — the IAP verification payload was read field by field and holds no birth date, age band or adult flag, so "paid from an adult's Apple ID" is not evidence the app can read; (b) `public.consent_records` records a PROCESSING purpose per family and has no notion of who was checked or how, so it could not carry this; (c) the attestation cannot carry it either — no method verifies PARENTHOOD, and the FTC-approved 2023 method establishes ADULTHOOD (a genuine photo ID, a live face matching it). | Lead: CONFIRMED, and split along the line of what each provider can actually establish, because the two halves are not equally available. FIX: PREMISE: the check must be un-bypassable and its outcome must be the server's, so the fix works only if (i) no partial result can be read as a pass by any caller present or future and (ii) nothing off the document is kept. Both are observable in the schema. Migration `0980_identity_verification.sql` adds `public.identity_verifications` with `adult_confirmed` GENERATED as `document_is_government_id and document_holder_is_adult and face_match = 'matched'` — so there is no configuration, and no later code change, that turns a partial check into a confirmation — and with NO column for an image, a face template, a document number, a date of birth or a name. `images_discarded_at` is NOT NULL, so a row cannot exist without asserting the deletion. `app.record_identity_verification` writes the attempt and, on a confirmation only, a `verified` `consent_records` row with method 'document_and_selfie', in ONE transaction — which is what makes every gate already in the product (the pairing-code route, the job gates, child access) start refusing without this check, instead of needing a new gate in each (L-037, by construction). The OpenAI adapter reads the document (a new `identity_document` AI stage) and ATTEMPTS the face comparison (`identity_face_compare`), recording whatever comes back; it declares `canCompareFaces: false` and `productionReadiness` blocks on it, so a deployment running only it refuses every family at the last step rather than passing them. It also applies its OWN ZDR precondition: the child-data gate does not cover an adult's licence, and an identity document sent to a provider that retains inputs sits in someone else's logs. | `supabase/tests/identity_verification.test.ts` 14 cases (the exact column list, the generated column refusing a write, all five non-matching face outcomes, the consent row written only on a confirmation and never for a refusal, the test-provider flag carried through, RLS read scope, and every write refused to authenticated/anon/child). `apps/api/tests/config.test.ts` 4 cases pin the readiness gate itself. MUTATION: nine in total, each red and each file restored with `sha256sum -c` — drop the face match from the generated expression; make `adult_confirmed` writable; write the consent row for every attempt; grant authenticated write on the table; let `failure_code` accept prose; call `openai_document` ready; allow the mock in production; delete the readiness item. One PASSED at first (an unrecognised provider name falling back to the mock) and the test's claim was corrected rather than the code: on a configuration error the Worker never starts, so that branch's return value is unreachable and asserting it was asserting dead code (L-054). | THE FACE COMPARISON IS NOT DONE and cannot be done with OpenAI: their usage policies prohibit biometric identification and the vision models decline the question, so this adapter records `refused` and no adult is ever confirmed by it. An identity vendor is an owner action; until one is configured, `productionReadiness` reports `identity_provider` blocked and the product cannot issue a child code. The biometric-law exposure (Illinois BIPA and its per-violation statutory damages, Texas CUBI, Washington) is also the vendor's to carry rather than PencilLift's, which is a second reason not to build the comparison here. |

IMPACT: a kindergarten child was given work a year above their grade in two of six subjects, against an explicit owner rule, and the existing test PINNED it — `expect(subjectGrade('grammar_writing', 0)).toBe(1)` inside a case whose own name said "(nearest grade when none exists)". | Ruled out before filing: (a) the isolation held everywhere else — `generateCandidates` bounds skills with `skillsForGrade(subject, subjectGrade(...))`, the composer only picks from those candidates and the planner passes the child's own grade, so no other path widened; (b) work BELOW grade is not the defect — the prerequisite list and the 'accessible' category draw from lower grades deliberately, and the rule is about work a child is not ready for, not about revision; (c) parent-supplied material is the parent's choice and is still built at the child's grade by `parentPassageItems`. | Lead: the owner's rule wins, and the owner AMENDED it the same day — "we can reach up or down 1 or 2" — which is what shipped. Both kindergarten pairs are one grade away, so both are served again — the change from the original behaviour is that the distance is BOUNDED, ASSERTED and stated in one place instead of being an open-ended "nearest supported grade". `subjectStartsAtGrade` is kept for the day a pair falls outside the reach, when the product must say "Spelling and vocabulary starts at grade 1" rather than show an empty subject; nothing needs that sentence today and the test says so. Removing the own-grade preference reds 3; widening the reach to 5 reds the case that pins the constant; making `subjectStartsAtGrade` count family material reds 2; and the earlier unbounded-reach restore reds 5. All restored with `sha256sum -c`. FOUR of my own assertions were wrong first and the TESTS were corrected rather than the code: one compared an item against its SKILL's teaching grade (curated reading bands by the PASSAGE, so a grade-0 passage may legitimately carry a grade-1 inference question); one forbade items whose band ends BELOW the child's grade, which would have banned the 'accessible' category — that is, banned revision; one wrote every assertion in terms of `SUBJECT_GRADE_REACH`, so setting the reach to 5 left the whole file green while a kindergartener got grade-5 work (the constant is now asserted literally); and one claimed a tie-break behaviour that is UNREACHABLE with today's bank — the only unserved pairs are at grade 0, which has nothing below it — so the test now records that mutating the search order reds nothing, and why. | No UI work is outstanding after the amendment: with the reach at two, no subject is empty at any grade, so there is no gap for a screen to explain. `subjectStartsAtGrade` and its test exist for the day one appears — widen the bank's grade span or narrow the reach and some pair falls out of range, at which point the planner must carry that sentence. A per-child opt-in to work further above grade remains the owner's to decide and is deliberately not built. |

| BUG-420 | Medium / forward compatibility — every future field addition breaks every installed app | **OPEN — accepted, not fixed, with a named trigger** | lead, measuring the concern raised while reviewing `closeAccountResponseSchema` | 97 response schemas in `packages/contracts` are `z.strictObject`, which THROWS on an unknown key rather than ignoring it. So the moment the server adds a field to a response, every already-installed build whose bundled schema predates it rejects the whole response — and the parent sees a successful call reported as an error. The concrete case that prompted this: `closeAccountResponseSchema`'s pending arm requires `reason`, which is not an additive change, so a shipped client parsing a 202 with the older strict schema would report a completed account closure as a failure. PencilLift ships to three stores where a meaningful share of users stay on one build for weeks | — | NOT FIXED, deliberately, and the reasoning is the decision rather than the excuse. Impact in beta is ZERO: there are no installed clients, so the defect cannot occur before the first public build. Rewriting 97 schemas touches every vertical for no beta benefit, and the strict parse is LOAD-BEARING in the suites — the portal's identity tests parse every fixture through the real schema precisely so a fixture the server could not send fails in the test rather than passing on a shape the server cannot produce. Changing runtime behaviour across the whole contract surface days before a beta, to pre-empt something that cannot happen during it, is the wrong trade. THE INTENDED FIX, for its own slice: requests stay `strictObject` (so no client can smuggle in the outcome it wants, which is the reason the identity contract's header gives and it is a good one); responses become `z.object`, which STRIPS unknown keys. That asymmetry is the standard one and the existing comment only ever justified the request half | Measured rather than asserted: 97 of 114 response/status/view schemas are strict, 0 are loose (`packages/contracts/src/*.ts`). No test covers the old-client case, because no old client exists to write one against | **TRIGGER: the first response-schema field addition after the first public build.** Until then this row is the whole mitigation. Recorded in `docs/Release_Readiness.md` so it is read at submission time rather than rediscovered by a parent |
